is unrooted), we have
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{d}(T(\underline{r},l,i))=\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{d}(T(\underline{r},l,i))
. The reason for the name “symmetric traversability” is that if
\mathcal{U}
has a finite number of stable directions and
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
is a symmetric droplet (recall
Section

3.3
), then, for any
l>0
,
i\in[4k]
,
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus T(\underline{r},l,i)}
and
{\eta}\in{\Omega}_{T(\underline{r},l,i)}
, we have

\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{d}(T(\underline{r},l,i))\Leftrightarrow\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{d}(T(\underline{r},l,i+2k)),

(16)

denoting by
{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus T(\underline{r},l,i+2k)}
the boundary condition obtained by rotating
{\omega}
by
\pi
around the center of
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
and similarly for
\eta^{\prime}
. To see this, recall from
Section

3.5.2
that
\mathcal{H}^{\omega}(S)\supset\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
with equality when
\alpha(u_{i})>\alpha
and note that the same symmetry as in
Eq.

16
holds at the level of the segment
S_{j,m}
and its symmetric one,
S^{\prime}_{j+2k,m}={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\cap{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+m\underline{v}_{i+2k}+{\rho}_{j+2k}\underline{e}_{j+2k})\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+m\underline{v}_{i+2k})
:

\eta\in\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}^{\omega}_{C^{2}+d}(S_{j,m})&\alpha(u_{j+2k})\leqslant\alpha\\
\mathcal{H}^{W}_{C^{2}+d}(S_{j,m})&\alpha(u_{j+2k})>\alpha\end{cases}\Leftrightarrow\eta^{\prime}\in\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{C^{2}+d}(S^{\prime}_{j+2k,m})&\alpha(u_{j})\leqslant\alpha\\
\mathcal{H}^{W}_{C^{2}+d}(S^{\prime}_{j+2k,m})&\alpha(u_{j})>\alpha,\end{cases}

all four cases following directly from
3.8
,
3.10
and
3.9
.

We next state a simple observation which is used frequently to modify boundary conditions as we like at little cost.

Lemma 4.2

(Changing boundary conditions)
.

Let
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet,
l>0
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
and
i\in[4k]
. Assume that for any
j\in[4k]\setminus\{i-k,i+k\}
the side length
s_{j}
of
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
satisfies
s_{j}\geqslant C^{3}
. Set
T=T(\underline{r},l,i)
. Then there exists a decreasing event
\mathcal{W}(T)\subset{\Omega}_{T}
such that
{\mu}(\mathcal{W}(T))\geqslant q^{O(W)}
for any
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus T}
and
\eta\in\mathcal{W}(T)
we have

{\eta}\in\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T)\Leftrightarrow{\eta}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T).

Moreover,
{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T))=q^{-O(W)}{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T))
for all
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus T}
. The same holds with
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}
instead of
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

Recall the segments
S_{j,m}
from
4.1
. Let
\mathcal{W}(T)
be the intersection of
\mathcal{H}^{W}_{C^{2}}(S_{j,m})
for the largest sufficiently large but fixed number of values of
m
for each
j\in(i-k,i+k)
, such that
\varnothing\neq S_{j,m}\subset T
. By
3.11

{\mu}(\mathcal{W}(T))\geqslant q^{O(W)}
. Moreover, the boundary condition is irrelevant for the remaining segments, so
\mathcal{W}(T)
is indeed as desired. Finally, by
Eq.

7
we have

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T)\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T)\right)\leqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{W}(T)\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{W}(T))}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant q^{-O(W)}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{W}(T)\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T)\right)\leqslant q^{-O(W)}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T)\right).\qed

Another convenient property allowing us to decompose a long tube into smaller ones is the following.

Lemma 4.3

(Decomposing tubes)
.

Let
T=T(\underline{r},l,i)
be a tube,
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus T}
be a boundary condition and
s\in[0,l]
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
. Set
T_{1}=T(\underline{r},s,i)
and
T_{2}=su_{i}+T(\underline{r},l-s,i)
. Then

{\eta}\in\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T(\underline{r},l,i))\Leftrightarrow\left({\eta}_{T_{2}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T_{2})\text{ and }{\eta}_{T_{1}}\in\mathcal{T}^{{\eta}_{T_{2}}\cdot{\omega}}(T_{1})\right)

and the same holds for
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}
instead of
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
4.1
, since for each of the segments
S_{j,m}
in
4.1
either
S_{j,m}\subset T_{1}
or
S_{j,m}\cap T_{1}=\varnothing
and similarly for
T_{2}
(see
Fig.

3(a)
).
∎

{\omega}
u_{i}
s
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
T(\underline{r},s,i)
u_{i+2k}
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})+xu_{i}
T(\underline{r},s-x,i)+xu_{i}
T(\underline{r},x,i+2k)+xu_{i}

(a)

East-extension. The thickened tube is traversable (
\mathcal{T}
).

{\omega}
{\omega}
u_{i}
u_{i+2k}
s-x
x
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})+xu_{i}
T(\underline{r},s-x,i)+xu_{i}
T(\underline{r},x,i+2k)+xu_{i}

(b)

CBSEP-extension. Thickened tubes are symmetrically traversable (
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}
).

Figure 3
:
One-directional extensions. The black droplet is SG. Helping sets appear on each line of the hatched parallelograms as indicated by the hatching direction. The white strips have width
\Theta(C^{2})
.

4.2
East-extension

We start with the East-extension (see
Fig.

3(a)
), which is simpler to state.

Definition 4.4

(East-extension)
.

Fix
i\in[4k]
, a droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
, a multiple
l>0
of
{\lambda}_{i}
and an event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}
. Assume that
{\alpha}(u_{j})<\infty
for all
j\in(i-k,i+k)
. We use the expression “
we East-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
” to state that, for all
s\in(0,l]
multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
and
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})}
, we define the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i}))\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})}
to occur for
{\eta}\in{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})}
if

{\eta}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\quad\text{and}\quad{\eta}_{T(\underline{r},s,i)}\in\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}}(T(\underline{r},s,i)).

In other words, given the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}
for the droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
, we define the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}
(in particular for
{\omega}=\mathbf{1}
, but not only) for the larger droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})={\Lambda}(\underline{r})\sqcup T(\underline{r},l,i)
. The event obtained on the larger droplet requires for the smaller one to be
\mathbf{1}
-super good (SG) and for the remaining tube to be
{\omega}
-traversable (recall
4.1
). Note that these two events are independent.
Further observe that if
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
is translation invariant (recall
3.13
), then so is
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i}))
for any
s\in(0,l]
multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
, defined by East-extending
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
. To get a grasp on
4.4
, let us note the following fact, even though it is not used directly in the proof of
1
.

Lemma 4.5

(East-extension ergodicity)
.

Let
i\in[4k]
,
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet,
l
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}
be an event. Assume that
{\alpha}(u_{j})<\infty
for all
j\in(i-k,i+k)
. Further assume that
\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
implies that the
\mathcal{U}
-KCM with initial condition
{\eta}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}
can entirely infect
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
. If we East-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
, then for any
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})}
and
\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
the
\mathcal{U}
-KCM with initial condition
{\omega}\cdot\eta
can entirely infect
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})
.

Proof.

The proof is rather standard, so we only sketch the reasoning. Let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
. Since
\eta_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
by
4.4
, by hypothesis we can completely infect
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
, starting from
{\omega}\cdot{\eta}
. We next proceed by induction on
s\in[0,l]
to show that we can infect
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})
. When a new site in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
is added to this set, as we increase
s
, we actually add to it an entire segment
S_{j,m}
as in
4.1
(at most one
m
for each
j\in(i-k,i+k)
). Since
T(\underline{r},l,i)
is
({\omega},0)
-traversable, by
3.10
and
4.1
, there is a helping set (in direction
u_{j}
) for this segment. As noted in
Section

3.5.1
, helping sets in direction
u_{j}
together with the half-plane
{\mathbb{H}}_{u_{j}}
infect the entire line
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{j}}\setminus{\mathbb{H}}_{u_{j}}
on the boundary of the half-plane. Since the helping set in our setting is only next to a finite fully infected droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})
, infection spreads along its edge until it reaches a bounded distance from the corners (see
[
7
]
*Lemma 3.4). However, by our choice of
\hat{}\mathcal{S}
(recall
Section

3.2
), for each
j\in[4k]
there is a rule
X\in\mathcal{U}
such that
X\subset\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{j}}\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{j+1}}
. Using this rule, we can infect even the remaining sites to fill up the corner between directions
u_{j}
and
u_{j+1}
of the droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s^{\prime}\underline{v}_{i})
with
s^{\prime}>s
minimal such that
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s^{\prime}\underline{v}_{i})\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})\neq\varnothing
(see
[
8
]
*Lemma 5.5 and Fig. 6).
∎

We next state a recursive bound on the Poincaré constant
{\gamma}
from
Section

3.6
reflecting the recursive definition of SG events in an East-extension. In rough terms, it states that in order to relax on the larger volume, we need to be able to relax on the smaller one and additionally pay the cost of creating logarithmically many copies of it shifted by exponentially growing offsets, conditionally on the presence of the original droplet. We further need to account for the cost of microscopic dynamics (see the
e^{\log^{2}(1/q)}
term below), but its contribution is unimportant. Recall
\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}
from
Section

3.4
.

Proposition 4.6

(East-extension relaxation)
.

Let
i\in[4k]
be such that for all
j\in(i-k,i+k)
we have
{\alpha}(u_{j})<\infty
. Let
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet with
\underline{r}=q^{-O(C)}
and side lengths at least
C^{3}
. Let
l\in(0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}]
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
. Define
d_{m}={\lambda}_{i}\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\rfloor
for
m\in[1,M)
and
M=\min\{m:{\lambda}_{i}(3/2)^{m}\geqslant l\}
. Let
d_{M}=l
,
{\Lambda}^{m}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}+d_{m}\underline{v}_{i})
and
s_{m-1}=d_{m}-d_{m-1}
for
m\in[2,M]
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
be a nonempty translation invariant decreasing event. Assume that we East-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
. Then
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
is also nonempty, translation invariant, decreasing and satisfies

{\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))\leqslant\max\left({\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r})),{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\right)\right)e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}\prod_{m=1}^{M-1}a_{m},

with

a_{m}={\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{m}+s_{m}u_{i}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right).

(17)

The proof is left to
Section

A.3
.

4.3
CBSEP-extension

We next turn our attention to CBSEP-extensions (see
Fig.

3(b)
). The definition differs from
4.4
(cf.
Fig.

3(a)
) in three ways. Firstly, we allow the smaller SG droplet to be anywhere inside the larger one (the exact position is specified by the offset below). Secondly, we ask for traversability on both sides of the smaller droplet in the direction away from it (so that infection can spread, starting from it), rather than just on one side. Thirdly, we require our tubes to be symmetrically traversable, instead of traversable. This makes the position of the small SG droplet roughly uniform.

Definition 4.7

(CBSEP-extension)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}
has a finite number of stable directions (equivalently,
\alpha(u_{j})<\infty
for all
j\in[4k]
). Fix
i\in[4k]
, a droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
and a multiple
l
of
{\lambda}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
be a translation invariant event. We use the expression “
we CBSEP-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
” to state that, for all
s\in(0,l]
multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
and
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})}
, we define the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i}))\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i})}
as follows.

For
offsets

x\in[0,s]
divisible by
{\lambda}_{i}
we define
{\eta}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i}))
if the following all hold:

\displaystyle\eta_{T(\underline{r},s-x,i)+xu_{i}}

\displaystyle{}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}}(T(\underline{r},s-x,i)+xu_{i});

\displaystyle{\eta}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})+xu_{i}}

\displaystyle{}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r})+xu_{i});

\displaystyle\eta_{T(\underline{r},x,i+2k)+xu_{i}}

\displaystyle{}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}}(T(\underline{r},x,i+2k)+xu_{i}).

We then set
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i}))=\bigcup_{x}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{x}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+s\underline{v}_{i}))
.

Note that CBSEP-extending in direction
u_{i}
gives the same result as CBSEP-extending in direction
u_{i+2k}
. We further reassure the reader that, in applications
4.4
and
4.7
, are not used simultaneously for the same droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
, so no ambiguity arises as to whether
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
is obtained by CBSEP-extension or East-extension. However, as it is clear from
Table

2(b)
, it is sometimes necessary to CBSEP-extend a droplet itself obtained by East-extending an even smaller one. But for the time being, let us focus on a single CBSEP-extension.

The following analogue of
4.5
holds for CBSEP-extension, which is also not used directly in the proof of
1
.

Lemma 4.8

(CBSEP-extension ergodicity)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}
has a finite number of stable directions. Let
i\in[4k]
,
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet and
l
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}
be translation invariant. Further assume that
\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
implies that the
\mathcal{U}
-KCM with initial condition
{\eta}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}
can entirely infect
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
. If we CBSEP-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
, then for any
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})}
and
\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
the
\mathcal{U}
-KCM with initial condition
{\omega}\cdot\eta
can entirely infect
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})
.

Proof.

By
4.7
, it suffices to prove that for each offset
x\in[0,s]
the conclusion holds for
\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{x}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
. By
4.7
, this implies that the events

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(xu_{i}+{\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(xu_{i}+T(\underline{r},s-x,i))
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(xu_{i}+{\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(xu_{i}+T(\underline{r},x,i+2k))
hold. Moreover, by
4.1
,
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}(T)\subset\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}(T)
for any tube
T
and boundary condition
{\omega}^{\prime}
. Therefore, we may apply
4.5
to each of the droplets
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+x\underline{v}_{i})
and
xu_{i}+{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+(s-x)\underline{v}_{i})
(in directions
u_{i}
and
u_{i+2k}
respectively) to obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

We next state the CBSEP analogue of
4.6
, which is more involved, but also more efficient. Roughly speaking, we show that the time needed in order to relax on a CBSEP-extended droplet, is the product of four contributions: the Poincaré constant of the smaller droplet; the inverse probability of the symmetric traversability events in
4.7
; the cost of microscopic dynamics; the conditional probability of suitable contracted versions of the super good and symmetric traversability events, given the original ones (recall
Section

2.3.1
). The last two contributions turn out to be negligible, but the last one requires some care and make the statement somewhat technical.

Proposition 4.9

(CBSEP-extension relaxation)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}
has a finite number of stable directions. Let
i\in[4k]
. Let
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet with
\underline{r}=q^{-O(C)}
and side lengths at least
C^{3}
. Let
l\in(0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}]
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
be a nonempty translation invariant decreasing event.

Denote
{\Lambda}_{1}=T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i+2k)
,
{\Lambda}_{2}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}-{\lambda}_{i}\underline{v}_{i})
and
{\Lambda}_{3}=T(\underline{r}-{\lambda}_{i}\underline{v}_{i},{\lambda}_{i},i)
, so that
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+{\lambda}_{i}\underline{v}_{i})-{\lambda}_{i}u_{i}={\Lambda}_{1}\sqcup{\Lambda}_{2}\sqcup{\Lambda}_{3}
and
{\Lambda}_{2}\cup{\Lambda}_{3}={\Lambda}(\underline{r})=({\Lambda}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2})+{\lambda}_{i}u_{i}
. Consider some nonempty decreasing events
4
4

4

We use a bar to denote “contracted” versions of events (recall
Section

2.3.1
).

\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2})\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{2}}({\Lambda}_{1})\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{2}}({\Lambda}_{3})\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}_{3}}
for all
{\eta}_{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2})
. Assume that

\left\{{\eta}:{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{1}}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}}({\Lambda}_{1}),{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}),{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{3}}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}}({\Lambda}_{3})\right\}\\
\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2})\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{2}\cup{\Lambda}_{3}).

(18)

Set
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2})=\{{\eta}:{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}),{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{1}}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}}({\Lambda}_{1})\}
.

If we CBSEP-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
, then
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
is nonempty, translation invariant, decreasing and satisfies

{\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))\leqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})))}\times\max\left({\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\right),{\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\right)\\
\times\frac{e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}}{{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2})|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2}))\min_{{\eta}_{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2})}{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{2}}({\Lambda}_{3})|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}({\Lambda}_{3}))}.

4.9
is proved in
Section

A.3
based on
[
23
]
. We referring the reader to
[
23
]
*Section
4.3 for the principles behind
4.9
in a less technical framework, but let us briefly discuss the contracted events.

Equation

18
should be understood as follows. In the middle droplet
{\Lambda}_{2}
, which has the shape of
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
, but contracted in direction
u_{i}
by
O(1)
, we require an event
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2})
. This event provides simultaneously as much of the structure of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2})
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{2}\cup{\Lambda}_{3})
(these regions both have the shape of
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
), as one can hope for, given that we are missing a tube of length
O(1)
of these regions. Once such a favourable configuration
{\eta}_{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2})
is fixed, the events
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}}({\Lambda}_{1})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{{\Lambda}_{2}}}({\Lambda}_{3})
provide exactly the missing part of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2})
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{2}\cup{\Lambda}_{3})
respectively. In applications, these events necessarily need to be defined, taking into account the structure of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
, on which we have made no assumptions at this point.

4.4
Conditional probability tools

In both
4.6
and
4.9
our bounds feature certain conditional probabilities of SG events. We now provide two tools for bounding them.

The next result generalises
[
23
]
*Corollary A.3, which relied on explicit computations unavailable in our setting. It shows that the offset of the core of a CBSEP-extended droplet (see
Fig.

3(b)
and recall the notation
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\omega}
from
4.7
) is roughly uniform. This result is the reason for the somewhat artificial
3.8
of helping sets and
4.1
of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}
(also see
2.1
).

Lemma 4.10

(Uniform core position)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}
has a finite number of stable directions. Fix
i\in[4k]
and a symmetric droplet
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})
obtained by CBSEP-extension by
l
in direction
u_{i}
. Assume that
l\leqslant\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}
is divisible by
{\lambda}_{i}
and that the side lengths of
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
are at least
C^{3}
. Then for all
s\in[0,l]
divisible by
{\lambda}_{i}
and
{\omega},{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}}

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{s}({\Lambda})\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda})\right)\geqslant q^{O(C)}.

The proofs of
4.10
and

are left to

. The latter vastly generalises
[

]

. In words,

states in a quantitative way that the conditional probability of a tube of “critical” size,

, being traversable, given that a slightly perturbed version of it (shifted spatially, with different boundary condition, width of the white strips in

, radii and length) is traversable, is not very low. We note that sizes other than the critical one are not important, so cruder bounds suffice.

Lemma 4.11

(Perturbing a tube)
.

Let
i\in[4k]
such that
{\alpha}(u_{j})\leqslant{\alpha}
for all
j\in(i-k,i+k)
. Let
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet with side lengths
\underline{s}
and let
T=T(\underline{r},l,i)
be a tube. Assume that
l\in[{\Omega}(1),e^{q^{-o(1)}}]
,
s=\min_{i-k<j<i+k}s_{j}=q^{-{\alpha}+o(1)}
and
\max_{i-k<j<i+k}s_{j}=q^{-{\alpha}+o(1)}
. For some
{\Delta}\in[C^{2},s/W^{2}]
, let
\underline{r}^{\prime}
and
l^{\prime}
be such that
0\leqslant s_{j}-s_{j}^{\prime}\leqslant O({\Delta})
for all
j\in(i-k,i+k)
and
0\leqslant l-l^{\prime}\leqslant O({\Delta})
, where
\underline{s}^{\prime}
are the side lengths of the droplet
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\prime})
. Further let
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be such that
\|x\|=O({\Delta})
and
d,d^{\prime}\in[0,O({\Delta})]
with
d\leqslant d^{\prime}
. Denoting
T^{\prime}=T(\underline{r}^{\prime},l^{\prime},i)+x
, for any boundary conditions
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus T}
and
{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus T^{\prime}}
, we have

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{d^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\right|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{d}(T)\right)\geqslant q^{O(W)}\left(1-(1-q^{\alpha})^{{\Omega}(s)}\right)^{O({\Delta})}\\
\times\left(1-W{\Delta}/s-q^{1-o(1)}\right)^{O(l)}.

5
Isotropic models

For this section we assume
\mathcal{U}
to be isotropic (class
(g)
). In this case the reasoning closely follows and generalises
[
23
]
. We treat internal and mesoscopic dynamics simultaneously, since for this class there is no difference between the two.

5.1
Isotropic internal and mesoscopic dynamics

We start by defining the geometry of our droplets and the corresponding length scales. They are all symmetric and every
2k
-th droplet is twice larger. Each such dilation is decomposed into
2k
steps, so that their geometry fits the setting of our CBSEP-extensions from
Section

4.3
(see
Fig.

5(a)
and recall
Fig.

3(b)
).

Recall
Section

3.3
and the constant
{\varepsilon}
from
Section

3.4
. Let
\underline{r}^{(0)}
be a sequence of radii with
r_{i}^{(0)}=r_{i+2k}^{(0)}
for all
i\in[2k]
, such that for all
i\in[4k]
,
r_{i}^{(0)}=\Theta(1/{\varepsilon})
and the corresponding side length
s_{i}^{(0)}=\Theta(1/{\varepsilon})
is a multiple of
2{\lambda}_{i+k}
. For any integer
m\geqslant 0
,
i\in[2k]
and
n=2km+r
with
r\in[2k]
we define

s_{i}^{(n)}=s_{i+2k}^{(n)}=s^{(0)}_{i}2^{m}\times\begin{cases}2&k\leqslant i<k+r\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(19)

and
{\Lambda}^{(n)}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(n)})
with
\underline{r}^{(n)}
the sequence of radii associated to
\underline{s}^{(n)}
satisfying
r_{i}^{(n)}=r_{i+2k}^{(n)}
for all
i\in[2k]
. Further set
N^{\mathrm{mes}+}=2k\lceil\log({\varepsilon}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+})/\log 2\rceil
(recall
\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}
from
Section

3.4
).

Note that, as claimed,
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
are nested symmetric droplets extended in one direction at each step satisfying
{\Lambda}^{(2km)}=2^{m}{\Lambda}^{(0)}
. Moreover, they are nested so that we can define their SG events by extension (recall
4.7
and
3(b)
for CBSEP-extensions).

Definition 5.1

(Isotropic SG)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be isotropic. We say that
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
is
SG
(
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
occurs), if all sites in
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
are infected. We then recursively define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})
for
n\in[N^{\mathrm{mes}+}]
by CBSEP-extending
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
in direction
u_{n}
by
l^{(n)}=s_{n+k}^{(n)}=\Theta(2^{n/2k}/{\varepsilon})
(recall from
Section

3.2
that indices of directions and sequences are considered modulo

as needed and see

5(a)
).

Recall from

.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be isotropic (class
(g)
). Then for all
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{mes}+}

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{\exp(1/(\log^{C/2}(1/q)q^{\alpha}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}))},

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-1}{q^{\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right).

The rest of
Section

5.1
is devoted to the proof of
5.2
. The bound on
{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))
is fairly standard in bootstrap percolation and could essentially be attributed to
[
7
]
, but we prove it in
5.6
, since we also need some better bounds on intermediate scales. Bounding
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})})
is more demanding and is done by iteratively applying
4.9
, as suggested by
5.1
.

Note that
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(0)})=1
, since
Eq.

15
is trivial, because
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
is a singleton. We seek to apply
4.9
, in order to recursively upper bound

for all
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{mes}+}
. To that end, we need the following definition of contracted events. Since, in the language of
4.9
, the events
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}_{{\eta}_{2}}
we define do not depend on
{\eta}_{2}
, we directly omit it from the notation.
