Lemma 5.4

(CBSEP-extension relaxation condition)
.

For all
n\in[N^{\mathrm{mes}+}]
we have
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})\times\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
and similarly for
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}
instead of
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

If
n<2k
, this follows directly from
5.3
, since
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})\times\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
is only the fully infected configuration and similarly for
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}
. We therefore assume that
n\geqslant 2k
and set
n=2km+r
with
r\in[2k]
.

We start with the first claim. Note that
{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}={\Lambda}^{(n)}
. Let
{\eta}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})\times\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
. We proceed by induction on
i
to show that
{\eta}_{{\Lambda}^{(i)}}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(i)})
for
i\in[n-2k,n]
.

The base is part of
5.3
. Assume
{\eta}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(i)})
for some
i\in[n-2k,n)
. Then by
4.7
, it suffices to check that

{\eta}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(i)},l^{(i)}/2,i\right)\right)\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(i)},l^{(i)}/2,i+2k\right)\right),

(21)

since then
{\eta}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{l^{(i)}/2}({\Lambda}^{(i+1)})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(i+1)})
.

Let us first consider the case
i=n-2k
and assume for concreteness that
m
is even (so that
u_{i}=u_{r}
). Then

\eta\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(i)},l^{(i)}/2-{\lambda}_{r},r\right)\right),

so by
4.3
it suffices to check that
{\eta}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(u_{i}(l^{(i)}/2-{\lambda}_{r})+T(\underline{r}^{(i)},{\lambda}_{r},i))
, in order for the first symmetric traversability event in
Eq.

21
to occur. We claim that this follows from
{\eta}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
and the fourth condition in
5.3
. To see this, notice that for each
j\in[4k]
the
u_{j}
-side length of
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(i)})
satisfies
s_{j}^{(i)}=\Theta(s_{j}^{(0)}2^{m})\gg 2^{m}/(W{\varepsilon})
by
Eq.

19
. Further recall from
Section

3.5
that
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)\subset\mathcal{H}^{\omega}(S)
for any segment
S
of length at least
C
and boundary condition
{\omega}
. Thus, for each of the segments in
4.1
for the tube
u_{i}(l^{(i)}/2-{\lambda}_{r})+T(\underline{r}^{(i)},{\lambda}_{r},i)\subset{\Lambda}^{(n)}
, we have supplied not only a helping set, but in fact several
W
-helping sets. For directions
u_{j}
with
j\in(r-k,r+k)\setminus\{r\}
, they are in
{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2}
, while for
j=r
they are found in
{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{3}
, if
k=1
and
m
is even, and in
{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2}
otherwise (see
Fig.

5(b)
). Hence, the claim is established. For the second event in
Eq.

21
the reasoning is the same except that when
k>1
or
m
is even, the tube
T(\underline{r}^{(i)},l^{(i)}/2,i+2k)
is entirely contained in
{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}
, so only
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})
is needed.

We next turn to the case
i\in(n-2k,n)
, which is treated similarly. Indeed,

{\eta}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}_{W}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(i)}-{\lambda}_{r}(\underline{v}_{r}+\underline{v}_{r+2k}),l^{(i)}/2,i\right)\right).

Comparing this tube to the desired one in
Eq.

21
,
T(\underline{r}^{(i)},l^{(i)}/2,i)
, we notice that the lengths and positions of their sides differ by
O(1)
(see
Fig.

4
). However, recalling
4.1
and
3(a)
, decreasing the width of each parallelogram there by
{\Omega}(W)\gg O(1)
(using the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}_{W}^{\mathbf{1}}
rather than
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}
) is enough to compensate for this discrepancy (the shaded zones in
Fig.

4
are empty in this case). It remains to ensure that the first and last
O(1)
segments in
4.1
also have helping sets. But this is guaranteed by the fourth condition in
5.3
and (depending on the values of
k
,
i
and
m
)
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
exactly as in the case
i=n-2k
.

Finally, the statement for
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}
is also proved analogously (with the offset for
i=n-2k
modified by
{\lambda}_{r}
in
Eq.

21
), so the proof is complete.
∎

By
5.4
,
Eq.

18
holds, so we may apply
4.9
. This gives

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\max\left({\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right),{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}))}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}\left({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}\left({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}\right)\right)

(22)

\displaystyle\times{\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}\left({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)

for
n\geqslant 2k
and
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(n)})\leqslant e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}
for
n<2k
. We therefore assume that
n\geqslant 2k
. Recalling
5.3
, note that both
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
can be guaranteed by the presence of
O(W^{2})
well chosen infected
W
-helping sets, since only
O(W)
disjoint segments of length
2^{m}/(W{\varepsilon})
perpendicular to
u_{j}
for a given
j\in(r-k,r+k)
can be fit in
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}
or
{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{3}
(see
Fig.

5(b)
), so it suffices to have a
W
-helping set at each end of those. This and the Harris inequality,
Eqs.

8
and
9
, give

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}\left({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}\left({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}\right)\right)\geqslant{\mu}\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}\left({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}\right)\right)\geqslant q^{W^{O(1)}},

(23)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}{\mu}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}\left({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)\\
\geqslant q^{W^{O(1)}}{\mu}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right).\end{multlined}

To deal with the last term we prove the following.

Lemma 5.5

(Contraction rate)
.

Setting
m=\lfloor n/(2k)\rfloor\geqslant 1
, we have

{\mu}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)\\
\geqslant\begin{cases}{\mu}\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)&2^{m}\leqslant 1/\left(\log^{C}(1/q)q^{\alpha}\right),\\
q^{O(C)}\frac{{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}))}{{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)}\right)\right)}&2^{m}\geqslant\log^{C}(1/q)/q^{\alpha},\\
\exp\left(-2^{m}q^{1-o(1)}\right)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(26)

Proof.

The first case follows from the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
.

For the other two cases we start by noting that
{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2}={\Lambda}^{(n)}-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}
may be viewed as a
2k
-fold CBSEP-extension of
{\Lambda}^{(n-2k)}
. Recalling the offset in
4.7
, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{0}

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{i}

\displaystyle{}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{l^{(n-j)}/2}\left({\Lambda}^{(n-j+1)}-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}\right)\qquad i{}\in[1,2k-1],

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{2k}

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{2k-1}\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{l^{(n-2k)}/2-{\lambda}_{r}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n-2k+1)}-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}\right),

so that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{i}
corresponds to fixing the position of the core, which is a translate of
{\Lambda}^{(n-i)}
, inside
{\Lambda}^{(n)}-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}
, but leaving its internal offsets unconstraint (see
Fig.

5(b)
). Thus,
4.10
applied
2k
times gives

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{2k}\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2}\right)\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{2k}{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{i}|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{i-1})\geqslant q^{O(C)}.

Expanding the definition of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{2k}
via
4.7
, we see that this event is the intersection of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)})
with some increasing events (symmetrically traversable tubes) independent of the latter. Thus, the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
gives

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant q^{O(C)}{\mu}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{2k}\right)

(27)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant q^{O(C)}{\mu}\left(\left.\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)}\right)\right).

Taking into account that
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)})
by
5.3
, this concludes the proof of the second case of
Eq.

26
.

For the third case, our starting point is again
Eq.

27
. This time we observe that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{2k}
can be written as the intersection of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)})
with
4k
symmetric traversability events, each of which is a perturbed version (in the sense of
4.11
and
4
) of the ones appearing in
5.3
of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})
. Thus, the Harris inequality
Eq.

9
allows us to lower bound
{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{2k})
by

\displaystyle{\mu}(\mathcal{W})\times{\mu}

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k)},l^{(n-2k)}/2-{\lambda}_{r},r\right)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\,\left|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k)},l^{(n-2k)}/2+{\lambda}_{r},r\right)-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times{\mu}

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k)},l^{(n-2k)}/2-{\lambda}_{r},r+2k\right)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\,\left|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k)},l^{(n-2k)}/2-{\lambda}_{r},r+2k\right)-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1}^{2k-1}\prod_{\xi=0}^{1}{\mu}

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}_{W}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k+i)}-{\lambda}_{r}\left(\underline{v}_{r}+\underline{v}_{r+2k}\right),l^{(n-2k+i)}/2,r+i+2k\xi\right)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\,\left|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k+i)},l^{(n-2k+i)}/2,r+i+2k\xi\right)-{\lambda}_{r}u_{r}\right)\right),

where
\mathcal{W}
is the event appearing in the last item of
5.3
.

Firstly, each of the above conditional probabilities is bounded by

q^{O(W)}\log^{-C^{O(1)}}(1/q)\left(1-q^{1-o(1)}\right)^{O(2^{m}/{\varepsilon})}\geqslant\exp\left(-2^{m}q^{1-o(1)}\right),

using
4.11
with
{\Delta}=C^{2}
and recalling that
2^{m}=q^{-{\alpha}}\log^{O(C)}(1/q)
and
{\alpha}\geqslant 1
. Secondly,
{\mu}(\mathcal{W})\geqslant q^{W^{O(1)}}
as in
Eq.

23
, concluding the proof of
Eq.

26
. We direct the reader to
[
23
]
*Appendix A for the details of an analogous argument in a simpler setting.
∎

Iterating
Eq.

22
and plugging
Eqs.

23
and
5.1
gives that
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})})
is at most

\frac{e^{O(C^{2})N^{\mathrm{mes}+}\log^{2}(1/q)}q^{2N^{\mathrm{mes}+}W^{O(1)}}}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}))}\prod_{n=2k}^{N^{\mathrm{mes}+}-1}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left(\left.{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}_{1}\cup{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)\right).

Further recalling that
N^{\mathrm{mes}+}=O(\log(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}))=O(C\log(1/q))
and inserting
Eq.

26
, we obtain

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}\right)\leqslant\frac{e^{q^{-{\alpha}+1-o(1)}}}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}))}\prod_{n=2k}^{2^{m}\leqslant 1/(\log^{C}(1/q)q^{\alpha})}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)\\
\times\prod_{n:2^{m}\geqslant\log^{C}(1/q)/q^{\alpha}}^{N^{\mathrm{mes}+}-1}\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)}))}{{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}))}.

(28)

with
m=\lfloor n/(2k)\rfloor
.
The final ingredient are the following probability bounds.

Lemma 5.6

(Probability of super good droplets)
.

For
n\in[2k,N^{\mathrm{mes}+}]
and
m=\lfloor n/(2k)\rfloor
, the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-1}{\log^{C-3}(1/q)q^{\alpha}}\right)

if

\displaystyle 2^{m}\leqslant\frac{1}{\log^{C}(1/q)q^{\alpha}},

(29)

\displaystyle\frac{{\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)}))}

\displaystyle{}\geqslant q^{W^{O(1)}}

if

\displaystyle 2^{m}\geqslant\frac{\log^{C}(1/q)}{q^{\alpha}},

(30)

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-1}{q^{\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right).

(31)

Proof.

Let us first bound
{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))
for
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{mes}+}
by induction, starting with the trivial bound

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(2k)}\right)\right)\geqslant q^{|{\Lambda}^{(2k)}|}\geqslant q^{O(1/{\varepsilon})}.

(32)

From
4.7
, translation invariance and
Eq.

16
, for
n\in[2k,N^{\mathrm{mes}+}-1]
we have

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}={\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right){\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n)},l^{(n)},n\right)\right)\right)

(33)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant q^{O(1/{\varepsilon})}\prod_{i=2k}^{n}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(i)},l^{(i)},i\right)\right)\right),

so we need to bound the last term. Applying
4.1
,
4.2
and the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
and then
3.11
, we get

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n)},l^{(n)},n\right)\right)\right)\geqslant{}

\displaystyle q^{O(W)}\prod_{j,m^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}_{C^{2}}\left(S_{j,m^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant{}

\displaystyle q^{O(W)}\left(1-e^{-q^{\alpha}2^{m}/O({\varepsilon})}\right)^{O(2^{m}/{\varepsilon})}

(34)

\displaystyle\geqslant{}

\displaystyle q^{O(W)}\begin{cases}\left(q^{\alpha}2^{m-1}\right)^{C2^{m}/{\varepsilon}}&2^{m}\leqslant 1/q^{{\alpha}}\\
\exp\left(-2^{m}\exp\left(-q^{\alpha}2^{m}\right)\right)&2^{m}>1/q^{{\alpha}},\end{cases}

where the product runs over the segments
S_{j,m^{\prime}}
appearing in
4.1
for the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T(\underline{r}^{(n)},l^{(n)},n))=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T(\underline{r}^{(n)},l^{(n)},n))
(the last equality holds, since
\mathcal{U}
is isotropic). Plugging
Eq.

34
into
Eq.

33
and iterating, we get

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)\geqslant\begin{cases}\exp\left(-1/\left(\log^{C-2}(1/q)q^{\alpha}\right)\right)&2^{m}\leqslant 1/\left(\log^{C}(1/q)q^{\alpha}\right)\\
\exp\left(-1/\left(q^{\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)\right)&2^{m}>1/\left(\log^{C}(1/q)q^{\alpha}\right)\end{cases}

(35)

since
N^{\mathrm{mes}+}\leqslant O(C)\log(1/q)
. This proves
Eq.

31
.

Recalling
5.3
, as in the proof of
5.5
, we have that for any
n\in[2k,N^{\mathrm{mes}+}]

{\mu}\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}\left({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\right)\right)={\mu}(\mathcal{W}){\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n-2k)}\right)\right)\\
\begin{aligned} \times{}&\prod_{\xi=0}^{1}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k)},l^{(n-2k)}/2-{\lambda}_{r},r+2k\xi\right)\right)\right)\\
\times{}&\prod_{\xi=0}^{1}\prod_{i=1}^{2k-1}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}_{W}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n-2k+i)}-{\lambda}_{r}\left(\underline{v}_{r}+\underline{v}_{r+2k}\right),l^{(n-2k)}/2,r+2k\xi\right)\right)\right),\end{aligned}

where
\mathcal{W}
is the event from the last item of
5.3
and
r=n-2km
. As in the proof of
5.5
, we have
{\mu}(\mathcal{W})\geqslant q^{W^{O(1)}}
, while the factors in the products can be bounded exactly as in
Eq.

34
, entailing
Eqs.

29
and
30
, since we already have
Eq.

35
.
∎

Proof of
5.2
.

The bound on
{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))
was proved in
Eqs.

31
and
32
. The one on
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})})
follows by plugging
Eqs.

30
and
29
into
Eq.

28
.
∎

5.2
CBSEP global dynamics

For the global dynamics we need to recall the global CBSEP mechanism introduced in
[
23
]
. It is useful not only for class
(g)
, but also other unrooted models—classes
(d)
and
(f)
.

Let
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}
and
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}
be droplets with side lengths
\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-})
and
\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+})
respectively (recall
Section

3.4
). Consider a tiling of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with square boxes
Q_{i,j}=[0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}})\times[0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}})+\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}(i,j)
for
(i,j)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

Definition 5.7

(Good and super good boxes)
.

We say that the box
Q_{i,j}
is
good
if for every segment
S\subset Q_{i,j}
, perpendicular to some
u\in\widehat{}\mathcal{S}
of length at least
{\varepsilon}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}
,
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
occurs (recall
3.9
). We denote the corresponding event by
\mathcal{G}_{i,j}
. We further say that
\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+})
occurs if for every segment
S\subset{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}
, perpendicular to some
u\in\widehat{}\mathcal{S}
of length at least
3{\varepsilon}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}
, the event
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
occurs.

Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-})\subset{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}}
be a nonempty translation invariant event. We say that
Q_{i,j}
is
super good
if it is good and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(x+{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-})
occurs for some
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
such that
x+{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}\subset Q_{i,j}
. We denote the corresponding event by
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}_{i,j}
.

In words, good boxes
Q_{i,j}
and droplets
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}
contain
W
-helping sets in sufficient supply for a SG translate of
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}
to be able to move inside the box or droplet containing it. Our choice of
\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}
makes being good so likely that we are able to assume that all boxes and droplets are good at all times. Finally, a box is SG, if it also contains a SG translate of
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}
that we wish to move around. Thus, when looking at SG boxes, we essentially see a two-dimensional CBSEP dynamics, which leads to the following bound.

Proposition 5.8

(Global CBSEP relaxation)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be unrooted (classes
(d)
,
(f)
and
(g)
). Let
T=\exp(\log^{4}(1/q)/q^{\alpha})
. Assume that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+})
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-})
are nonempty translation invariant decreasing events such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

(1-{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-})))^{T}T^{4}=o(1)
;

(2)

for all
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
such that
x+{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}\subset{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}
we have

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(x+{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-})\cap\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}).

Then

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mu}}[{\tau}_{0}]\leqslant{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}\right)\frac{\log(1/{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-})))}{q^{O(C)}}.

We omit the proof, which is identical to
[
23
]
*Section 5, given
5.7
,
6
6

6

Due to the difference between
Eq.

15
and
[
23
]
*Eq. (4.5), the factor
{\mu}_{{\Lambda}_{i,j}}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}_{i,j}))
in
[
23
]
*last display of Section 5 cancels out with
\pi(\mathcal{S}_{1})^{-1}
in
[
23
]
*Eq. (5.11) up to a
q^{O(C)}
factor, rather than compensating the conditioning in
[
23
]
*last display of Section 5, which is absent in our setting.
and turn to the proof of
1
for the isotropic class
(g)
.

Proof of
1
(g)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be isotropic. Recall the droplets
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
from
Section

5.1
. Set
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}={\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}
,
N^{\mathrm{mes}-}=2k\lceil\log({\varepsilon}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-})/\log 2\rceil
and
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}={\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}-})}
. Thus, the side lengths of
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}
and
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}
are indeed
\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-})
and
\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+})
respectively by
Eq.

19
. By
5.2
, condition
(1)
of
5.8
is satisfied:

\displaystyle(1-{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-})))^{T}T^{4}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant(1-e^{-1/(q^{\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{2})})^{T}T^{4}\leqslant T^{4}e^{-e^{\log^{4}(1/q)/q^{\alpha}-1/(q^{\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{2})}}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant e^{4\log^{4}(1/q)/q^{\alpha}-e^{\log^{4}(1/q)/(2q^{\alpha})}}=o(1).

We next seek to verify condition
(2)
. Proceeding by induction on
n\in[N^{\mathrm{mes}-},N^{\mathrm{mes}+}]
, it suffices to show that for any
n\in[N^{\mathrm{mes}-},N^{\mathrm{mes}+})
and
x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
such that
x+{\Lambda}^{(n)}\subset y+{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\subset{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}
, we have

\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+})\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(x+{\Lambda}^{(n)})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(y+{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}).

(36)

Recalling
5.1
and
4.7
, we see that it suffices to show that for any tube
T
of the form
z+T(\underline{r}^{(n)},l,j)
for some
l>0
,
j\in[4k]
and
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
satisfying
T\subset y+{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}
also verifies
\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T)
. Further recalling
4.1
, we see that it suffices to show that on
\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+})
, each segment of length
\min_{j\in[4k]}s_{j}^{(n)}-C^{2}-O(1)
perpendicular to
u_{j}
for some
j\in[4k]
contains an infected
W
-helping set (recall from
Section

3.5.2
that
\mathcal{H}^{W}_{d}(S)\subset\mathcal{H}^{\omega}_{d}(S)
). Hence,
Eq.

36
follows from
5.7
, since

\min_{j\in[4k]}s_{j}^{(n)}-C^{2}-O(1)=\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-})\geqslant 3{\varepsilon}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}.

Thus, we may apply
5.8
. Further plugging the bounds from
5.2
, we recover

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mu}}[{\tau}_{0}]

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{\exp(1/(\log^{C/2}(1/q)q^{\alpha}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}))}\frac{1}{q^{\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{O(C)}}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{\exp(1/(\log^{C/3}(1/q)q^{\alpha}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}+})}))}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{1+o(1)}{{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}}\right),

concluding the proof.
∎

6
Unbalanced unrooted models

In this section we assume
\mathcal{U}
is unbalanced unrooted (class
(d)
). We deal with the internal, mesoscopic and global dynamics separately. The internal dynamics is very simple and already known since
[
21
]
. The mesoscopic and global ones are similar to the ones of
Section

5
with some adaptations needed for the mesoscopic one.

6.1
Unbalanced internal dynamics

For unbalanced unrooted
\mathcal{U}
(class
(d)
) the SG event on to the internal scale consists simply in having an infected ring of thickness
W
(see
Fig.

6
). Recall
\ell^{\mathrm{int}}
from
Section

3.4
.

Definition 6.1

(Unbalanced unrooted internal SG)
.

Assume
\mathcal{U}
is unbalanced unrooted. Let
{\Lambda}^{(0)}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(0)})
be a droplet with side lengths
s_{j}^{(0)}=2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{int}}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil
for
j\in[4k]
. We say that
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
, is
super good
(
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
occurs) if all sites in
{\Lambda}^{(0)}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(0)}-W\underline{1})
are infected.

The following result was proved in
[
21
]
*Lemma 4.10 and provides the main contribution to the scaling for this class (see
Table

2(b)
).

Proposition 6.2
.

For unbalanced unrooted
\mathcal{U}
(class
(d)
) we have

\max\left({\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(0)}\right),{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(0)}\right)\right)\right)\leqslant q^{-O(W\ell^{\mathrm{int}})}\leqslant\exp\left(C^{3}\log^{2}(1/q)/q^{\alpha}\right).

6.2
CBSEP mesoscopic dynamics

Since
\mathcal{U}
is unbalanced unrooted, we may assume w.l.o.g. that
{\alpha}(u_{j})\leqslant{\alpha}
for all
j\in[4k]\setminus\{k,-k\}
. We only use
4k
scales for the mesoscopic dynamics. Recall
Sections

3.3
and
3.4
. For
i\in[0,2k]
let
{\Lambda}^{(i)}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(i)})
be the symmetric droplet centered at
0
with
\underline{r}^{(i)}
such that its associated side lengths are

s_{j}^{(i)}=s_{j+2k}^{(i)}=\begin{cases}2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{int}}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil&i-k\leqslant j<k\\
2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil&-k\leqslant j<i-k.\end{cases}

For
i\in(2k,4k]
, we define
{\Lambda}^{(i)}
similarly by

s_{j}^{(i)}=s_{j+2k}^{(i)}=\begin{cases}2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil&i-3k\leqslant j<k\\
2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil&-k\leqslant j<i-3k.\end{cases}

(37)

These droplets are exactly as in
Fig.

5(a)
, except that the extensions are much longer. More precisely, we have
{\Lambda}^{(i+1)}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(i)}+l^{(i)}(\underline{v}_{i}+\underline{v}_{i+2k})/2)
with
l^{(i)}=s_{i+k}^{(i+1)}-s_{i+k}^{(i)}
, so that
l^{(i)}=(1-q^{C-{\alpha}+o(1)})\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}
if
i\in[2k]
and
l^{(i)}=(1-O({\delta}))\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}
if
i\in[2k,4k)
. In particular, the droplets
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
for
n\in[4k+1]
are nested in such a way that allows us to define their SG events by extension, as in
5.1
(also recall
6.1
for
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
and
4.7
and
3(b)
for CBSEP-extensions).

Definition 6.3

(Unbalanced unrooted mesoscopic SG)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be unbalanced unrooted. For
n\in[4k]
we define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})
by CBSEP-extending
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
by
l^{(n)}
in direction
u_{n}
.

With this definition we aim to prove the following (recall
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(4k)})
from
Section

3.6
).

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be unbalanced unrooted (class
(d)
). Then

\max\left({\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(4k)}\right),{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(2k)}\right)\right)\right)\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/q)}{{\delta}q^{\alpha}}\right).

The remainder of
Section

6.2
is dedicated to the proof of
6.4
. Naturally,
6.4
results from
4k
applications of
4.9
and using
6.2
as initial input. The second step is somewhat special (see
Fig.

6(a)
), since there we need to take into account the exact structure of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
from
6.1
in the definition of the contracted events appearing in
4.9
. For the remaining steps the reasoning is identical to the proof of
5.2
, but computations are simpler, since there are only boundedly many scales. Following the proof of
5.2
, we start by defining our contracted events (cf.
5.3
).

(a)

Case
n=1
. The tube
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
contains
W
-helping sets close to its boundaries except the one perpendicular to
u_{k}
.

(b)

Case
n=2
. Regions around all boundaries contain
W
-helping sets.

Figure 6
:
The events
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
of
6.5
.
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
is thickened. Black regions are entirely infected. Shaded tubes are
(\mathbf{1},W)
-symmetrically traversable.

Definition 6.5

(Contracted unbalanced unrooted events)
.

For
n=2km+r\in[4k+1]
and
r\in[2k]
, define
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)},{\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2},{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
by
Eq.

20
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(0)}_{1})
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(0)}_{3})
) be the events that
{\Lambda}^{(0)}_{1}
(resp.
{\Lambda}^{(0)}_{3}
) is fully infected and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}^{(0)}_{2})
be the event that
{\Lambda}^{(0)}_{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(0)}-2W\underline{1})
is fully infected.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}^{(1)}_{2})
occur if the following all hold (see
Fig.

6(a)
):
5

•

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}(T(\underline{r}^{(0)}-{\lambda}_{1}\underline{v}_{1},l^{(0)}/2,0))
occurs,

•

({\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(0)}+W\underline{1})\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(0)}-2W\underline{1}))\cap{\Lambda}_{2}^{(1)}
is fully infected,

•

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}(T(\underline{r}^{(0)}-{\lambda}_{1}\underline{v}_{1},l^{(0)}/2,2k))
occurs,

•

for all
j\neq\pm k
and segment
S\subset{\Lambda}_{2}^{(1)}
, perpendicular to
u_{j}
at distance at most
W
from the
u_{j}
-side of
{\Lambda}_{2}^{(1)}
and of length
\ell^{\mathrm{int}}/W
, the event
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
occurs.

Further let
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{1}^{(1)})
occur if the following both hold (see
Fig.

6(a)
):

•

{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(0)}+W\underline{1})\cap{\Lambda}_{1}^{(1)}
is fully infected,

•

for all
j\neq\pm k
and segment
S\subset{\Lambda}_{1}^{(1)}
perpendicular to
u_{j}
of length
\ell^{\mathrm{int}}/W
the event
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
occurs.

We define
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(1)})
analogously.

Let
i\in[2,4k)
. We say that
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(i)}_{1})
occurs (see
Fig.

6(b)
) if for all
j\in[4k]
and
m\in\{i-1,i\}
every segment
S\subset{\Lambda}^{(i)}_{1}
perpendicular to
u_{j}
of length
s_{j}^{(m)}/W
at distance at most
W
from the
u_{j}
-side (parallel to
S
) of
{\Lambda}^{(m)}
, the event
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
occurs. We define
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(i)}_{3})
similarly. Let
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}^{(i)}_{2})
occur if the following all hold (see
Fig.

6(b)
):

•

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(i-2)})
occurs;

•

for each
m\in\{0,2k\}
the following occurs

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(i-2)},l^{(i-2)}/2-\sqrt{W},i-2+m\right)\right)\\
\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(i-1)}-\sqrt{W}\left(\underline{v}_{i}+\underline{v}_{i+2k}\right),l^{(i-1)}/2-\sqrt{W},i-1+m\right)\right);

•

for all
j\in[4k]
,
m\in\{i-2,i-1,i\}
and segment
S\subset{\Lambda}_{2}^{(i)}
, perpendicular to
u_{j}
of length
s_{j}^{(m)}/W
at distance at most
W
from the
u_{j}
-side of
{\Lambda}^{(m)}
, the event
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
holds.

Before moving on, let us make a few comments on how
6.5
of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}_{1})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}_{3})
is devised. Recall that our goal is to satisfy
Eq.

18
, that is,
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})\times\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)})
, so as to apply
4.9
. For that reason, for the various values of
n
, we have required the (more than) parts of the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)})
which can be witnessed in each of
{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}
and
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
. Since
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
corresponds to an infected ring of width roughly
W
and radius being fully infected (see
6.1
), we have required for
n\in\{0,1\}

a ring of the same radius, but three times thicker to be infected. Similarly to
5.3
, we have slightly reduced the length of traversable tubes present in (recall
6.3
), but thinned the corresponding parallelograms in
Fig.

3(b)
. We have further asked for
W
-helping sets around all boundaries so as to compensate for the shortening of the tubes. The construction takes advantage of the fact that for
n\geqslant 2
the droplet
{\Lambda}^{(n-2)}
is far from the boundaries of
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
(see
Fig.

6(b)
), so the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n-2)})
can be directly incorporated into
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}_{2})
, rather than being decomposed into one part in
{\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}
and one in
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
.

Lemma 6.6

(CBSEP-extension relaxation condition)
.

For all
n\in[4k]
we have
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})\times\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
and similarly for
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}
instead of
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

The proof for
n\geqslant 2
is essentially identical to the one of
5.4
and
n=0
is immediate from
6.5
and
6.1
. We therefore focus on the case
n=1
and on
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(1)}
, since
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(1)}
is treated analogously. Assume
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(1)})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(1)})
occur. Recalling
4.7
, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{l^{(0)}/2}({\Lambda}^{(1)})
occurs.

Firstly, note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(0)},l^{(0)}/2,2k\right)\right)\supset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}\left(T\left(r^{(0)}-{\lambda}_{1}\underline{v}_{1},l^{(0)}/2,2k\right)\right),

recalling from
Eq.

10
that
\langle\underline{v}_{1},\underline{e}_{j}\rangle=0
for all
j\in\{k+1,\dots,3k-1\}
and
\langle\underline{v}_{1},\underline{e}_{j}\rangle\leqslant O(1)\ll W
for
j\in\{k,3k\}
. Similarly, for any
\eta\in{\Omega}_{{\Lambda}^{(1)}}
we have

\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(0)}-{\lambda}_{1}\underline{v}_{1},l^{(0)}/2,0\right)\right)\Rightarrow\eta\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\eta_{{\Lambda}^{(1)}\setminus T}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{(1)}}}(T),

where
T=(\underline{r}^{(0)},l^{(0)}/2-{\lambda}_{1}/\langle u_{1},u_{0}\rangle,0)
. Furthermore, the fourth condition in the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(1)})
and the second condition in the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(1)})
(see
6.5
) imply the occurrence of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(u_{0}(l^{(0)}/2-{\lambda}_{1}/\langle u_{1},u_{0}\rangle)+T(\underline{r}^{(0)},{\lambda}_{1}/\langle u_{1},u_{0}\rangle,0))
. Using
4.3
to combine these two facts, we obtain that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(\underline{r}^{(0)},l^{(0)}/2,0)
occurs.

Thus, it remains to show that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
occurs. But, in view of
6.1
, this is the case by the second condition in the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(1)})
and the first condition of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(1)})
(see
6.5
).
∎

Proof of
6.4
.

By
6.6
,
Eq.

18
holds, so we may apply
4.9
. Together with the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
, this gives

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(4k)}\right)\leqslant\frac{{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(0)})\exp(O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q))}{\prod_{i\in[4k]}{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(i+1)})){\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(i)}_{1})){\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(i)})){\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(i)}_{3}))}.

(38)

In view of
6.2
, in order to prove
6.4
, it suffices to prove that each of the terms in the denominator of
Eq.

38
is at least
\exp(-C^{O(1)}\log^{2}(1/q)/q^{\alpha})
.

Inspecting
6.5
and
6.3
, we see that each
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}
,
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}
event in
Eq.

38
requires at most
C\ell^{\mathrm{int}}
fixed infections,
W^{O(1)}

W
-helping sets and
O(1)

(\mathbf{1},W)
-symmetrically traversable tubes. We claim that the probability of each tube being
(\mathbf{1},W)
-symmetrically traversable is
q^{O(W)}
. Assuming this, the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
and the above give that, for all
i\in[4k+1]
,

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(i)}\right)\right)\geqslant q^{C\ell^{\mathrm{int}}}q^{W^{O(1)}}q^{O(W)}=\exp\left(-C^{O(1)}\log^{2}(1/q)/q^{\alpha}\right)

and similarly for the other events.

To prove the claim, let us consider for concreteness and notational convenience the event

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}_{W}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(1)},l^{(1)},1\right)\right),

all tubes being treated identically. As in
Eq.

34
, applying
4.1
,
4.2
and
3.11
, we get

{\mu}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant q^{O(W)}\left(1-e^{-q^{\alpha}\ell^{\mathrm{int}}/O(1)}\right)^{O(l^{(1)})}\left(1-e^{-q^{W}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}/O(W)}\right)^{O(l^{(1)})}.

(39)

Here we noted that in directions
i\in(-k+2,k-1)
symmetric traversability only requires helping sets (since the only hard directions are assumed to be
u_{k}
and
u_{-k}
) and the corresponding side lengths of
{\Lambda}^{(1)}
are
\ell^{\mathrm{int}}+O(1)
, while for
i=k
it requires
W
-helping sets, but the
u_{k}
-side of
{\Lambda}^{(1)}
has length
\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}+O(1)
. Recalling
Section

3.4
and the fact that
l^{(1)}=\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-})
,
Eq.

39
becomes
{\mu}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant q^{O(W)}
,
as claimed.
∎

6.3
CBSEP global dynamics

With
6.4
established, we are ready to conclude the proof of
1
(d)
as in
Section

5.2
.

Proof of
1
(d)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be unbalanced unrooted. Recall the droplets
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
from
Section

6.2
. Set
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}={\Lambda}^{(4k)}
and
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}={\Lambda}^{(2k)}
. Condition
(1)
of
5.8
is satisfied by
6.4
, while condition
(2)
is verified as in
Section

5.2
.

Thus,
5.8
applies and, together with
6.4
, it yields

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mu}}[{\tau}_{0}]\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}q^{\alpha}}\right),

concluding the proof.
∎

7
Semi-directed models

In this section we aim to treat semi-directed update families
\mathcal{U}
(class
(f)
). The internal dynamics (
Section

7.1
) based on East extensions is the most delicate. The mesoscopic and global dynamics (
Sections

7.2
and
7.3
) use the CBSEP mechanism along the same lines as in
Sections

5
and
6
.

7.1
East internal dynamics

In view of
1.6
, in
Section

7.1
we work not only with semi-directed models (class
(f)
), but slightly more generally, in order to also treat balanced rooted models with finite number of stable directions (class
(e)
), whose update rules are contained in the axes of the lattice (in which case
k=1
—recall
Section

3.2
). In either case we have that
{\alpha}(u_{j})\leqslant{\alpha}
for all
j\in[4k]\setminus\{3k-1,3k\}
and this is the only assumption on
\mathcal{U}
we use.

Recalling
Section

3.4
, set

\displaystyle N^{\mathrm{cr}}

\displaystyle{}=\min\{n:W^{n}\geqslant q^{-{\alpha}}\}=\lceil{\alpha}\log(1/q)/\log W\rceil,

\displaystyle N^{\mathrm{int}}

\displaystyle{}=\min\left\{n:\left\lceil W^{\exp(n-N^{\mathrm{cr}})}/q^{\alpha}\right\rceil\geqslant\ell^{\mathrm{int}}{\varepsilon}\right\},

\displaystyle{}=N^{\mathrm{cr}}+\log\log\log(1/q)+O(\log\log W),

(40)

\displaystyle\ell^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}=\begin{cases}W^{n}&n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}},\\
\left\lceil W^{\exp(n-N^{\mathrm{cr}})}/q^{\alpha}\right\rceil&N^{\mathrm{cr}}<n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}.\end{cases}

Remark 7.1
.

Note that despite the extremely fast divergence of
\ell^{(n)}q^{\alpha}
, for
n\in(N^{\mathrm{cr}},N^{\mathrm{int}}]
it holds that
W\leqslant\ell^{(n+1)}/\ell^{(n)}<(\ell^{(n)}q^{\alpha})^{2}<\log^{4}(1/q)
. The sharp divergence ensures that some error terms below sum to the largest one. This prevents additional factors of the order of
N^{\mathrm{int}}-N^{\mathrm{cr}}
in the final answer, particularly for the semi-directed class
(f)
(recall
Section

2.4.3
). This technique was introduced in
[
24
]
*Eq. (16), while the geometrically increasing scale choice relevant for small
n
originates from
[
17
]
. It should be noted that this divergence can be further amplified up to a tower of exponentials of height linear in
n-N^{\mathrm{cr}}
. In that case the
\log\log\log(1/q)
error term in
8.5
and
4
below becomes
\log_{*}(1/q)
, but is, alas, still divergent.

Recall
Section

3.3
. Let
\underline{r}^{(0)}=(r^{(0)}_{j})_{j\in[4k]}
be a symmetric sequence of radii such that
\underline{r}=\Theta(1/{\varepsilon})
, the vertices of
{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(0)})
are in
2{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and the corresponding side lengths
\underline{s}^{(0)}
are also
\Theta(1/{\varepsilon})
. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
j\in[4k]
, we define
s_{j}^{(n)}=s_{j}^{(0)}\ell^{(n)}
. We denote
{\Lambda}^{(n)}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(n)})
, where
\underline{r}^{(n)}
is the sequence of radii corresponding to
\underline{s}^{(n)}
such that
r^{(n)}_{3k}=r_{3k}^{(0)}
and
r^{(n)}_{3k-1}=r^{(0)}_{3k-1}
(see
Fig.

9
).

l^{(1)}
l^{(1+1/4)}
l^{(1+2/4)}
l^{(1+3/4)}
{\Lambda}^{(2)}
{\Lambda}^{(1)}
{\Lambda}^{(0)}

Figure 9
:
Geometry of the nested droplets
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
for
k=2
in the setting of
Section

7.1
. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
droplets are symmetric and homothetic to the black
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
. Intermediate ones
{\Lambda}^{(1+1/4)}
,
{\Lambda}^{(1+2/4)}
and
{\Lambda}^{(1+3/4)}
obtained by East-extensions (see
Fig.

3(a)
) in directions
u_{0}
,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
respectively are drawn in progressive shades of grey.

For
j\in[2k]
, we write
l^{(n+j/(2k))}=s_{j+k}^{(n+1)}-s_{j+k}^{(n)}=\Theta(\ell^{(n+1)}/{\varepsilon})
and set
\underline{r}^{(n+(j+1)/(2k))}=\underline{r}^{(n+j/(2k))}+l^{(n+j/(2k))}\underline{v}_{j}
, which is consistent with the definition of
\underline{r}^{(n+1)}
above. Thus, denoting
{\Lambda}^{(n+j/(2k))}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(n+j/(2k))})
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
j\in(0,2k)
(see
Fig.

9
), we may define SG events of these droplets by extension (recall
4.4
and
3(a)
for East-extensions).

Definition 7.2

(Semi-directed internal SG)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be semi-directed or balanced rooted with finite number of stable directions and
k=1
. We say that
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
is SG (
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
occurs), if all sites in
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
are infected. We then recursively define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+(j+1)/(2k))})
, for
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}}]
and
j\in[2k]
, by East-extending
{\Lambda}^{(n+j/(2k))}
in direction
u_{j}
by
l^{(n+j/(2k))}
(see
Fig.

9
).

As usual, we seek to bound the probability of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
and associated
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
(recall
Section

3.6
).

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be semi-directed (class
(f)
) or balanced rooted with finite number of stable directions (class
(e)
) and
k=1
. Then

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{6}q^{\alpha}}\right),

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-1}{{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}}\right).

The rest of
Section

7.1
is dedicated to the proof of
7.3
. The probability bound is fairly easy, as in
Eq.

31
, while the relaxation time is bounded by iteratively using
4.6
and then carefully estimating the product appearing there with the help of
4.11
.

Note that
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(0)})=1
, since
Eq.

15
is trivial, as
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
is a singleton. For
n\in 1/(2k){\mathbb{N}}
,
j\in[2k]
and
m\geqslant 1
, such that
n<N^{\mathrm{int}}
and
n-j/(2k)\in{\mathbb{N}}
set

a^{(n)}_{m}={\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}+\left(\left\lfloor(3/2)^{m+1}\right\rfloor-\left\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\right\rfloor\right){\lambda}_{j}u_{j}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right).

(41)

We further let

M^{(n)}=\min\left\{m:{\lambda}_{j}(3/2)^{m+1}\geqslant l^{(n)}\right\}=\log l^{(n)}/\log(3/2)+O(1).

(42)

For the sake of simplifying expressions we abusively assume that
l^{(n)}={\lambda}_{j}\lfloor(3/2)^{M^{(n)}+1}\rfloor
. Without this assumption, one would need to treat the term corresponding to
m=M^{(n)}
below separately, but identically.

We next seek to apply
4.6
with
\underline{r}=\underline{r}^{(n)}
and
l=l^{(n)}
. Let us first analyse the term
a_{m}
in
Eq.

17
. By
4.4
and the Harris inequality
Eq.

9
, we have

a_{m}\leqslant\frac{a_{m}^{(n)}}{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T+(\lfloor(3/2)^{m+1}\rfloor-\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\rfloor){\lambda}_{j}u_{j})|\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T))}=\frac{a_{m}^{(n)}}{b_{m}^{(n)}},

(43)

using
4.3
in the equality and setting

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle{}=T\left(\underline{r}^{(n)},{\lambda}_{j}\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\rfloor,j\right)

\displaystyle b_{m}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}={\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n)},\left(\left\lfloor(3/2)^{m+1}\right\rfloor-\left\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\right\rfloor\right){\lambda}_{j},j\right)\right)\right)

Moreover, by
4.3
and
4.2
we have

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}b_{m}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}=q^{-O(WM^{(n)})}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(n)},l^{(n)},j\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}=q^{-O(WM^{(n)})}\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1/(2k))}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))},

(44)

where the second equality uses
7.2
and
4.4
.

Applying
4.6
successively and using
Eqs.

43
and
42
, we get

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\max_{n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}\frac{a_{m}^{(n)}}{b_{m}^{(n)}}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)}))e^{O(C^{2})N^{\mathrm{int}}\log^{2}(1/q)}}{{\mu}^{2}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}))}\prod_{n=0}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}q^{-O(WM^{(n)})}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{\exp(\log^{O(1)}(1/q))}{{\mu}^{2}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}))}\prod_{n=0}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)},

(45)

where in the second inequality we used
Eq.

44
and the fact that
{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))
is non-increasing in
n
(recall
7.2
and
4.4
); in the third inequality we used
N^{\mathrm{int}}\leqslant\log(1/q)
by
Eq.

40
and
M^{(n)}\leqslant O(\log(1/q))
by
Eqs.

42
and
40
. Note that in
Eq.

45
and below products on
n
run over
1/(2k){\mathbb{N}}
.

To evaluate the r.h.s. of
Eq.

45
we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
n\in 1/(2k){\mathbb{N}}
be such that
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}
and
m\geqslant 1
. Then

a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)\leqslant\min\left(({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{n})^{-W^{n}/{\varepsilon}^{2}},e^{1/({\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha})}\right).

(46)

Moreover, if

\displaystyle\ell^{(\lfloor n\rfloor)}

\displaystyle{}\geqslant 1/\left(q^{{\alpha}}\log^{W}(1/q)\right),

\displaystyle M^{(n)}{}

\displaystyle\geqslant m+W,

\displaystyle(3/2)^{m}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant 1/q^{\alpha},

(47)

setting

n_{m}=\min\left\{n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}:\ell^{(n^{\prime})}\geqslant 1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right),M^{(n^{\prime})}\geqslant m+W\right\}\leqslant n,

(48)

the following improvements hold

\displaystyle a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{(3/2)^{m}}{{\varepsilon}^{4}}\left((N^{\mathrm{cr}}-n_{m})^{2}+{\mathbbm{1}}_{n\geqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}}}\log^{2/3}\log(1/q)\right)\right)

(49)

\displaystyle\times\begin{cases}\exp\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}(1/q)\right)\right)&m\leqslant\frac{\log(1/(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)))}{\log(3/2)}\\
\exp\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}\log(1/q)\right)\right)&m>\frac{\log(1/(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)))}{\log(3/2)}.\end{cases}

Let us finish the proof of
7.3
before proving
7.4
.

Proof of
7.3
.

The second inequality in
7.3
is contained in
Eq.

46
, so we focus on
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
based on
Eq.

45
. Set

M_{\alpha}=\left\lceil\log(1/q^{\alpha})/\log(3/2)\right\rceil.

(50)

Using the trivial bound
a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant\exp(1/({\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}))
from
Eq.

46
and then
Eqs.

40
and
42
, we get

\displaystyle\prod_{n=N^{\mathrm{cr}}-\lceil 1/{\varepsilon}\rceil}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}\prod_{m=M_{\alpha}}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{2}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\sum_{n=N^{\mathrm{cr}}}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}\left(M^{(n)}-M_{\alpha}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{n=N^{\mathrm{cr}}}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}O(1+\log(\ell^{(\lceil n+1/(2k)\rceil)}q^{\alpha}/{\varepsilon}))}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{n=N^{\mathrm{cr}}}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}}e^{n+1-N^{\mathrm{cr}}}}{{\varepsilon}^{4}q^{\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{5}q^{\alpha}}\right),

(51)

which is the main contribution. Note that by
Eqs.

42
and
40
,
n<N^{\mathrm{cr}}-1/{\varepsilon}
implies
M^{(n)}<M_{\alpha}
, so
Eq.

51
exhausts the terms in
Eq.

45
with
m\geqslant M_{\alpha}
.

Next set

N_{W}=\left\lceil-\log\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right)/\log W\right\rceil.

(52)

Using the first bound on
a_{m}^{(n)}
from
Eq.

46
and
Eq.

42
, we obtain

\displaystyle\prod_{n=0}^{N_{W}}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\prod_{n=0}^{N_{W}}({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{n})^{-O(\log(1/q)W^{n}/{\varepsilon}^{2})}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(-\log^{O(1)}(1/q)\sum_{n=0}^{N_{W}}W^{n}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}(1/q)\right)\right).

(53)

We next turn to the range
N_{W}\leqslant n<n_{m}
with
m<M_{\alpha}
. Recalling
Eqs.

48
,
42
and
40
, we get that
N_{W}\leqslant n<n_{m}
implies
M^{(n)}<m+W
and therefore
l^{(n)}\leqslant O((3/2)^{m+W})
, so
W^{n}\leqslant(3/2)^{m}
. Plugging this into the first bound on
a_{m}^{(n)}
from
Eq.

46
, we get

\displaystyle\prod^{M_{\alpha}-1}_{m=1}\prod_{n=N_{W}}^{n_{m}-1/(2k)}a_{m}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(-\sum^{M_{\alpha}}_{m=1}\frac{(3/2)^{m}\log({\delta}q^{\alpha}(3/2)^{m})}{{\varepsilon}^{3}}\right)\leqslant e^{1/(q^{\alpha}{\varepsilon}^{4})}.

(54)

It remains to treat the range
n_{m}\leqslant n<N^{\mathrm{int}}
with
m<M_{\alpha}
. Note that by
Eqs.

52
,
48
and
40

N_{W}\leqslant n_{m}
for any
m
and set

M_{W}=\left\lfloor\log\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right)\right)/\log(3/2)\right\rfloor.

(55)

Then
Eq.

49
gives

\displaystyle\sum^{M_{\alpha}-1}_{m=1}\sum_{n=n_{m}}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}\log a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\frac{2k}{{\varepsilon}^{4}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{\alpha}-1}(3/2)^{m}(N^{\mathrm{cr}}-n_{m})^{2}(N^{\mathrm{int}}-N^{\mathrm{cr}}+N^{\mathrm{cr}}-n_{m})

\displaystyle+\frac{2k}{{\varepsilon}^{4}}(N^{\mathrm{int}}-N^{\mathrm{cr}})\log^{2/3}\log(1/q)\sum_{m=1}^{M_{\alpha}-1}(3/2)^{m}

\displaystyle+\frac{2kM_{\alpha}N^{\mathrm{int}}}{q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}(1/q)}+\frac{2k(M_{\alpha}-M_{W})(N^{\mathrm{int}}-N_{W})}{q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}\log(1/q)}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\frac{8k}{{\varepsilon}^{4}}\log\log\log(1/q)\sum_{m=1}^{M_{\alpha}-1}(3/2)^{m}(N^{\mathrm{cr}}-n_{m})^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{\log^{2/3}\log(1/q)\log\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{5}q^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}(1/q)}+\frac{1}{q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}\log(1/q)},

(56)

where we used that
N^{\mathrm{int}}-N^{\mathrm{cr}}\leqslant 2\log\log\log(1/q)
by
Eq.

40
,
M_{\alpha}\leqslant\log^{O(1)}(1/q)
by
Eq.

50
,
N^{\mathrm{int}}\leqslant\log^{O(1)}(1/q)
by
Eq.

40
,
M_{\alpha}-M_{W}\leqslant\log^{O(1)}\log(1/q)
by
Eqs.

50
and
55
and
N^{\mathrm{int}}-N_{W}\leqslant\log^{O(1)}\log(1/q)
by
Eqs.

40
and
52
. In order to bound the last sum in
Eq.

56
, we note that by
Eqs.

55
,
48
,
40
and
50
, for any
m\in[M_{W},M_{\alpha})
we have
N^{\mathrm{cr}}-n_{m}\leqslant(M_{\alpha}-m)/{\varepsilon}
. Plugging this back into
Eq.

56
, we get

\displaystyle\sum^{M_{\alpha}-1}_{m=1}\sum_{n=n_{m}}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1/(2k)}\log a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\frac{\log\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{O(1)}}\left(M_{\alpha}^{4}(3/2)^{M_{W}}+(3/2)^{M_{\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\log^{3/4}\log(1/q)}{2q^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\frac{\log^{3/4}\log(1/q)}{q^{\alpha}}.

Plugging the last result and
Eqs.

51
,
7.1
and
54
in
Eq.

45
, we conclude the proof of
7.3
, since
{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}))\geqslant e^{-1/({\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha})}
by
Eq.

46
.
∎

Proof of
7.4
.

Let us fix
m
and
n
as in the statement for
Eq.

46
. The bound
a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{\mu}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))
follows from the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
. To upper bound the latter term we note that by
7.2
and
4.4
,

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)={\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(0)}\right)\right)\prod_{p=0}^{n-1/(2k)}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(p)},l^{(p)},j(p)\right)\right)\right),

(57)

setting
j(p)\in[2k]
such that
p-j(p)/(2k)\in{\mathbb{N}}
and letting products on
p
run over
1/(2k){\mathbb{N}}
. Clearly,

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(0)}\right)\right)=q^{|{\Lambda}^{(0)}|}=q^{\Theta(1/{\varepsilon}^{2})}.

(58)

Let us fix
p\in 1/(2k){\mathbb{N}}
,
p<N^{\mathrm{int}}
. Then,
using
4.2
,
4.1
,
3.11
, and the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
, we get

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(p)},l^{(p)},j(p)\right)\right)\right)\\
\begin{aligned} \geqslant{}&q^{O(W)}\left(1-e^{-q^{\alpha}\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor)}/O({\varepsilon})}\right)^{O(l^{(p)})}\\
\geqslant{}&q^{O(W)}\begin{cases}({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{p})^{W^{p}/({\delta}{\varepsilon})}&p\leqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}},\\
\exp\left(-1/\left(q^{{\alpha}}\exp\left(W^{\exp(\lfloor p\rfloor-N^{\mathrm{cr}})}/{\delta}\right)\right)\right)&p>N^{\mathrm{cr}}.\end{cases}\end{aligned}

(59)

In the last inequality we took into account
1/{\varepsilon}\gg 1/{\delta}\gg W\gg 1
,
\ell^{(N^{\mathrm{cr}})}=W^{O(1)}/q^{\alpha}
and the explicit expressions
Eq.

40
. From
Eqs.

58
,
57
and
59
it is not hard to check
Eq.

46
(recalling
Section

3.4
).

We next turn to proving
Eq.

49
, so we fix
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}
and
m\geqslant 1
satisfying
Eq.

47
. Denote
s_{m}=(\lfloor(3/2)^{m+1}\rfloor-\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\rfloor){\lambda}_{j}u_{j}
for
j=j(n)
, so that
Eq.

41
spells

a_{m}^{(n)}={\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}+s_{m}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right).

By the Harris inequality,
Eqs.

8
and
9
,
4.4
and
7.2
we have

\displaystyle a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n_{m})}\right)\right)

(60)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{p=n_{m}}^{n-1/(2k)}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(p)},l^{(p)},j(p)\right)+s_{m}\right)\right|\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(p)},l^{(p)},j(p)\right)\right)\right).

Our goal is then to bound the last factor, using
4.11
, which quantifies the fact that “small perturbations
s_{m}
do not modify traversability much.”

Let us fix
p\in[n_{m},n)\cap(1/2k){\mathbb{N}}
and denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(p)},l^{(p)},j(p)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\prime}

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{(p)},l^{(p)},j(p)\right)+s_{m}\right).

In order to apply
4.11
with
{\Delta}=\max(C^{2},\|s_{m}\|)
, we check that
W^{3}(3/2)^{m}\leqslant\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor)}/{\varepsilon}
(so that the sides of
{\Lambda}^{(p)}
are large enough). If
\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor)}\geqslant 1/q^{\alpha}
, this follows from the assumption
(3/2)^{m}\leqslant 1/q^{\alpha}
of
7.4
. Otherwise, by
Eqs.

48
and
42

\displaystyle W^{3}(3/2)^{m}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant(3/2)^{M^{(n_{m})}-W/2}\leqslant l^{(n_{m})}/e^{{\Omega}(W)}=\Theta\left(\ell^{(n_{m}+1)}\right)/\left({\varepsilon}e^{{\Omega}(W)}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\ell^{(n_{m})}/{\varepsilon}\leqslant\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor)}/{\varepsilon},

where in the last but one inequality we used that
\ell^{(n_{m}+1)}\leqslant W^{O(1)}\ell^{(n_{m})}
, since
n_{m}\leqslant p
and
\ell^{(p)}\leqslant 1/q^{\alpha}
(recall
Eq.

40
). The remaining hypotheses of
4.11
are immediate to verify.

For
\|s_{m}\|=\Theta((3/2)^{m})\leqslant C^{2}
,
4.11
gives

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\right|\mathcal{T}\right)\geqslant q^{O(C^{2})}\left(1-q^{1-o(1)}\right)^{O(l^{(p)})}\geqslant\exp\left(-q^{-{\alpha}+1/2}\right),

as
l^{(p)}\leqslant\ell^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}/{\varepsilon}\leqslant q^{-{\alpha}-o(1)}
. If, on the contrary,
\|s_{m}\|\geqslant C^{2}
,
4.11
gives

\displaystyle{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}|\mathcal{T})\geqslant{}

\displaystyle q^{O(W)}\times\left(1-(1-q^{\alpha})^{\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor)}/O({\varepsilon})}\right)^{O((3/2)^{m})}

(61)

\displaystyle\times\left(1-O(W{\varepsilon})(3/2)^{m}/\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor)}-q^{1-o(1)}\right)^{O(\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor+1)}/{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\geqslant{}

\displaystyle q^{O(W)}\times\begin{cases}({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{p})^{O((3/2)^{m})}&p\leqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}}\\
\exp\left(-(3/2)^{m}\exp\left(-W^{\exp(\lfloor p\rfloor-N^{\mathrm{cr}})}/{\delta}\right)\right)&p>N^{\mathrm{cr}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\times\begin{cases}\exp\left(-q^{-{\alpha}+1/2-o(1)}\right)&(3/2)^{m}\leqslant q^{-{\alpha}+1/2-o(1)}\\
\exp\left(-W^{2}(3/2)^{m}\frac{\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor+1)}}{\ell^{(\lfloor p\rfloor)}}\right)&(3/2)^{m}>q^{-{\alpha}+1/2-o(1)},\end{cases}

in view of
Eq.

40
. Further notice that if
(3/2)^{m}\leqslant q^{-{\alpha}+1/2-o(1)}
or
p>N^{\mathrm{cr}}
, the third term dominates, while otherwise the second one does. Moreover, if
p\geqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}}+\lceil\Psi\rceil
with

\Psi=\log\frac{\log\log\log(1/q)}{3\log W},

(62)

then the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
, translation invariance and
Eq.

59
directly give the bound

{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}|\mathcal{T})\geqslant{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})={\mu}(\mathcal{T})\geqslant\exp\left(-1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}\log(1/q)\right)\right).

(63)

Finally, we can plug
Eqs.

46
,
61
and
63
in
Eq.

60
to obtain the following bounds.
If
(3/2)^{m}\leqslant q^{-{\alpha}+1/2-o(1)}
, then

a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant\exp\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right)\right),

because the contribution from
Eq.

61
is negligible, since
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}\leqslant\log(1/q)
, while by
Eqs.

48
and
40
,
W^{n_{m}}=\ell^{(n_{m})}\leqslant W/(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q))
.
If, on the contrary,
(3/2)^{m}>q^{-{\alpha}+1/2-o(1)}
, then

\displaystyle a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\exp\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}(1/q)\right)\right)&(3/2)^{m}\leqslant 1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right)\\
\left({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{n_{m}}\right)^{-(3/2)^{m}/{\varepsilon}^{3}}&(3/2)^{m}>1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right)\end{cases}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{p=n_{m}}^{\min(n,N^{\mathrm{cr}})}({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{p})^{-O((3/2)^{m})}

\displaystyle{}\times\begin{cases}1&n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}}\\
\exp\left((3/2)^{m}W^{2\exp(\Psi)}/{\delta}\right)&n>N^{\mathrm{cr}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\times\begin{cases}\exp\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}(1/q)\right)\right)&(3/2)^{m}\leqslant 1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right)\\
\exp\left(1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W-O(1)}\log(1/q)\right)\right)&(3/2)^{m}>1/\left(q^{\alpha}\log^{W}(1/q)\right),\end{cases}

the terms corresponding to
{\mu}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n_{m})}))
and to values of
p
in the intervals
[n_{m},N^{\mathrm{cr}}]
,
(N^{\mathrm{cr}},N^{\mathrm{cr}}+\lceil\Psi\rceil)
and
[N^{\mathrm{cr}}+\lceil\Psi\rceil,N^{\mathrm{int}})
respectively. Indeed, in the last term for small
m
we used
Eq.

61
, while for large
m
, we directly applied
Eq.

63
. Observing that the product of the second case for the first term, the second term and the third term can be bounded by

\exp\left(\frac{(3/2)^{m}}{{\varepsilon}^{4}}\left((N^{\mathrm{cr}}-n_{m})^{2}+{\mathbbm{1}}_{n\geqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}}}\log^{2/3}\log(1/q)\right)\right),

we obtain the desired
Eq.

49
.
∎

7.2
CBSEP mesoscopic dynamics

In this section we assume that
\mathcal{U}
is semi-directed (class
(f)
) and w.l.o.g.
{\alpha}(u_{i})\leqslant{\alpha}
for all
i\in[4k]\setminus\{3k\}
. The approach to the mesoscopic dynamics is very similar to the one of
Section

6.2
, employing a bounded number of CBSEP-extensions to go from the internal to the mesoscopic scale. Once again, the geometry of our droplets is as in
Fig.

5(a)
, but extensions are much longer so that we go from scale
\ell^{\mathrm{int}}
to
\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}
in
2k
extensions and then to
\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}
in another
2k
extensions.

Recall from
Section

7.1
that we defined
{\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}
, a symmetric droplet with side lengths
\underline{s}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}
equal to
\Theta(\ell^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}/{\varepsilon})
, as well as

. Further recall

. Following

, for
i\in[1,2k]
we define

s^{(i+N^{\mathrm{int}})}_{j}=s^{(i+N^{\mathrm{int}})}_{j+2k}=\begin{cases}s_{j}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}&i-k\leqslant j<k,\\
2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil&-k\leqslant j<i-k,\end{cases}

while for
i\in(2k,4k]
, we set

s_{j}^{(i+N^{\mathrm{int}})}=s_{j+2k}^{(i+N^{\mathrm{int}})}=\begin{cases}2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil&i-3k\leqslant j<k\\
2{\lambda}_{j}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}/(2{\lambda}_{j})\rceil&-k\leqslant j<i-3k.\end{cases}

(64)

We then define
{\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i)}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i)})
with
\underline{r}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i)}
the sequence of radii associated to
\underline{s}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i)}
satisfying

\displaystyle{\Lambda}\left(\underline{r}^{(N_{i}+i)}\right)

\displaystyle{}={\Lambda}\left(\underline{r}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i-1)}+l^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i-1)}\left(\underline{v}_{i-1}+\underline{v}_{i+2k-1}\right)/2\right),

\displaystyle l^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i-1)}

\displaystyle{}=s^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i)}_{i+k-1}-s^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i-1)}_{i+k-1}=\begin{cases}(1-o(1))\ell^{\mathrm{mes}-}&i\in[1,2k],\\
(1-O({\delta}))\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}&i\in(2k,4k].\end{cases}

We then define the corresponding SG events by CBSEP-extension as in
6.3
.

Definition 7.5

(Semi-directed mesoscopic SG)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be semi-directed. For
i\in[4k]
we define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i+1)})
by CBSEP-extending
{\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i)}
by

in direction

.

We then turn to bounding
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+4k)})
(recall

3.6
).

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be semi-directed (class
(f)
). Then

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+4k)}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{O(1)}q^{\alpha}}\right),

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+2k)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-1}{{\varepsilon}^{O(1)}q^{\alpha}}\right).

The rest of
Section

7.2
is dedicated to the proof of
7.6
. The proof proceeds exactly like

, except that the first two steps are much more delicate. Namely, they require taking into account the internal structure of

) and also not symmetric w.r.t. the reflection interchanging
u_{0}
and

. This is not unexpected and is, to some extent, the crux of semi-directed models.

As before, we define

by

for

touches the boundary of

for all

(compare

and

).

Lemma 7.9
.

For all
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}},N^{\mathrm{int}}+4k)
we have
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)})\times\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(n)}\cup{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)})
and similarly for
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(n)}
instead of
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(n)}
.

Proof.

For
n\geqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}+2
the proof is the same as in
6.6
and
5.4
.

Assume that
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
occur. We seek to prove by induction that for all
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}
the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)})
occurs. For
n=0
this is true, since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and the corresponding part of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
in
7.7
give that
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
is fully infected. By
7.2
and
4.4
, it remains to show that for all
n<N^{\mathrm{int}}
the event
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T(\underline{r}^{(n)},l^{(n)},j))
occurs, where
j\in[4k]
is such that
n-j/(2k)\in{\mathbb{N}}
. But by
7.7
the corresponding event
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}(T(\underline{r}^{\prime(n)},l^{(n)},j)
occurs, where
{\Lambda}^{\prime(n)}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\prime(n)})
. It therefore remains to observe that
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, the
W
-helping sets in the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}_{3}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
imply
\mathcal{T}
. Indeed,
W
-helping sets ensure the occurrence of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{1}}_{C^{2}}(S)
for the first and last
\Theta(W)
segments
S
in
4.1
for
\mathcal{T}
, while the remaining ones are provided by
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, since
\underline{r}^{\prime(n)}
and
\underline{r}^{(n)}
only differ by
O(1)\ll W
. We omit the details, which are very similar to those in the proof of
6.6
(see
Fig.

10(a)
).

The remaining three cases (
{\Lambda}_{1}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}
instead of
{\Lambda}_{3}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}
and/or
N^{\mathrm{int}}+1
instead of
N^{\mathrm{int}}
) are treated analogously (see
Fig.

10
).
∎

Proof of
7.6
.

By
7.9
,
Eq.

18
holds, so we may apply
4.9
. Together with the Harris inequality,
Eqs.

7
and
8
, this gives

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+4k)}\right)\\
\leqslant\frac{{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})\exp(O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q))}{\displaystyle\prod_{i=N^{\mathrm{int}}}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}+4k-1}{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(i+1)})){\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(i)}_{1})){\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}}({\Lambda}_{2}^{(i)})){\mu}(\overline{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}}({\Lambda}^{(i)}_{3}))}.

(65)

In view of
7.3
, it remains to bound each of the terms in the denominator by
\exp(-1/({\varepsilon}^{O(1)}q^{\alpha}))
in order to conclude the proof of
7.6
.

Notice that a total of
{\varepsilon}^{-O(1)}
fixed infections and
W^{O(1)}N^{\mathrm{int}}=q^{o(1)}

W
-helping sets are required in all the events in
Eq.

65
. This amounts to a negligible factor. The probability of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}({\Lambda}^{\prime(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}({\Lambda}^{\prime\prime(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
can be bounded exactly like
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
in
7.4
. This yields a contribution of
\exp(1/({\varepsilon}^{O(1)}q^{\alpha}))
. Finally, the remaining bounded number of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}_{W}
events are treated as in
6.4
to give a negligible
q^{-O(W)}
factor. Hence, the proof of
7.6
is complete.
∎

7.3
Global CBSEP dynamics

The global dynamics is also based on the CBSEP mechanism and proceeds as in
Sections

5.2
and
6.3

Proof of
1
(f)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be semi-directed. Recall the droplets
{\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+i)}
for
i\in[4k+1]
from
Section

7.2
. Set
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}+}={\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+4k)}
and
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}-}={\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}}+2k)}
. Condition
(1)
of
5.8
is satisfied by
7.6
, while condition
(2)
is verified as in
Section

5.2
.

Thus,
5.8
applies and, together with
7.6
it yields

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mu}}[{\tau}_{0}]\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{O(1)}q^{\alpha}}\right),

concluding the proof.
∎

8
Balanced rooted models with finite number of stable directions

In this section we deal with balanced rooted models with finite number of stable directions (class
(e)
). The internal dynamics (
Section

8.1
) uses a two-dimensional version of East-extensions. As usual, it requires the most work, but applies directly also to balanced models with infinite number of stable directions (class
(b)
). The mesoscopic and global dynamics are imported from
[
21
]
in
Section

8.2
.

8.1
East internal dynamics

In this section we simultaneously treat balanced rooted models (classes
(b)
and
(e)
). We may therefore assume that
{\alpha}(u_{j})\leqslant{\alpha}
for all
j\in[-k+1,k]
and this is the only assumption on
\mathcal{U}
we use.

Let us start by motivating the coming two-dimensional East-extension we need. By the above assumption on the difficulties, we are allowed to use East-extensions in directions
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
. Indeed, recalling
4.4
, we see that for these directions the traversability events (recall
4.1
) only require helping sets and not
W
-helping sets. In principle, one could alternate East-extensions in these two directions similarly to what we did e.g. in
Section

7.1
for directions
u_{0},\dots,u_{2k-1}
. However, this would not work, because extensions in directions
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
only increase the length of the sides parallel to
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
, while all others remain unchanged (see
Fig.

3(a)
). Thus, the traversability events would be too unlikely, since they would require helping sets also for the other sides, e.g. the one with outer normal
u_{2-k}
, which are too small. This would make the probability of the SG event too large. Notice that this issue does not arise when
k=1
, as we saw in
Section

7.1
.

For
k>1
, however, we therefore need to make the
u_{j}
-sides of our successive droplets grow for all
j\in[-k+1,k]
. A natural way to achieve this is as depicted in
Fig.

13
. The drawback is that we can no longer achieve this directly with one-directional East-extensions as in
4.4
and
3(a)
, so we need some more definitions. However, morally, one such two-dimensional extension can be achieved by two East-extensions in the sense that, East-extending in direction
u_{0}
and then
u_{1}
yields a droplet which contains the desired droplet as in
Fig.

13
. Unfortunately, our approach heavily relies on not looking at the configuration outside the droplet itself. For that reason we instead need to find for each point in the droplet appropriate lengths of the East-extensions in directions
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
, so as to cover the point without going outside the target droplet (see
Fig.

14
).

{\Lambda}^{(2)}
{\Lambda}^{(1)}
{\Lambda}^{(0)}

•

For all

if

.

To construct the

and define

thanks to the fact that

and

. We direct the interested reader to

*Appendix E for the explicit details of a similar construction in arbitrary dimension.

Definition 8.1

(
n
-traversability)
.

Fix
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}}]
and let
R\subset{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}
be a region of the form

\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left(\bigcap_{{\kappa}\in I}D_{\kappa}\setminus\bigcup_{{\kappa}\in[K]\setminus I}D_{\kappa}\right)

(66)

for some family
\mathcal{I}
of subsets of
[K]
. We say that
R
is
n
-
traversable
(
\mathcal{T}_{n}(R)
occurs
7
7

7

The
n
-traversability
\mathcal{T}_{n}
should not be confused with
({\omega},d)
-traversability
\mathcal{T}_{d}^{\omega}
from
4.1
, which only features with
d=0
and
{\omega}=\mathbf{1}
in the present section.
) if for all
j\in(-k,k)
and every segment
S\subset R
perpendicular to
u_{j}
of length at least
{\delta}\ell^{(n)}/{\varepsilon}
the following two conditions hold.

•

If
S
is at distance at least
W
from the boundary of all
D_{{\kappa}}
, then the event
\mathcal{H}(S)
occurs.

•

If
S
is at distance at most
W
from a side of a
D_{{\kappa}}
parallel to
S
for some
{\kappa}\in[K]
, but
S
does not intersect any non-parallel side of any
D_{{\kappa}^{\prime}}
, then the event
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
occurs.

Roughly speaking,
R
must be one of the polygonal pieces into which the boundaries of all
D_{\kappa}
cut
{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}
. It is
n
-traversable, if segments of the size slightly smaller than
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
contain helping sets for the directions in
(-k,k)
. However, we only require this slightly away from the boundaries of
D_{\kappa}
and instead add
W
-helping sets close to boundaries, so that we can still cross them but keep the following independence.

Remark 8.2
.

Note that
n
-traversability events are product over the disjoint regions into which all the boundaries of
(D_{\kappa})_{{\kappa}\in[K]}
partition
{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}
.

Definition 8.3

(Two-dimensional East-extension)
.

For
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}}]
we say that we
East-extend

{\Lambda}^{(n)}
to
{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}
if
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1})
is defined by East-extending
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
by
l_{1}
in direction
u_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1})\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\setminus D_{1})
.

Indeed,
8.1
gives
\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\setminus D_{1})
, since
Eq.

66
is satisfied:

{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\setminus D_{1}=\bigcup_{{\kappa}\in[K]}D_{\kappa}\setminus D_{1}=\bigcup_{I\subset[K]\setminus\{0,1\}}\left(\bigcap_{{\kappa}\in I}D_{\kappa}\setminus\bigcup_{{\kappa}\not\in I}D_{\kappa}\right).

Armed with this notion, we are ready to define our SG events up to the internal scale for our models of interest.

Definition 8.4

(Balanced rooted internal SG)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be balanced rooted. We say that
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
is SG (
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)}
occurs), if all sites in
{\Lambda}^{(0)}
are infected. We then recursively define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})
for
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}}]
by East-extending
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
to
{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}
(see
8.3
).

We are now ready to state our bound on the probability of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
and
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
(recall
Section

3.6
).

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be balanced rooted (classes
(b)
and
(e)
). Then

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log(1/q)\log\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right),

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-1}{{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}}\right).

The rest of
Section

8.1
is dedicated to the proof of
8.5
. As usual, the probability bound is not hard (see
8.7
below), while the relaxation time is bounded recursively. However, we need to obtain such a recursive relation, using
4.6
twice (see
8.6
below). Yet, thanks to the additional
\log(1/q)
factor as compared to
7.3
(and the
\log\log\log(1/q)
one, see
1.6
), the computations need not be as precise and, in particular, do not rely on
4.11
.

Note that
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(0)})=1
, since
Eq.

15
is trivial, as
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(0)})
is a singleton. For
m\geqslant 1
and
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}}]
denote

a^{(n)}_{m}=\max_{j\in\{0,1\}}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}+\left(\lfloor(3/2)^{m+1}\rfloor-\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\rfloor\right){\lambda}_{j}u_{j}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right).

(67)

For the sake of simplifying expressions we abusively assume that for all
{\kappa}\in[K]
the length
l_{\kappa}
is of the form
{\lambda}_{0}\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\rfloor
with integer
m
. Without this assumption, one would need to treat the term corresponding to
m=M-1
in
4.6
separately, but identically. We next deduce
8.5
from the following two lemmas.

Lemma 8.6
.

For
n<N^{\mathrm{int}}
we have

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\leqslant\frac{{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(n)})e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}}{({\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})){\mu}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})))^{O(1)}}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)},

where
M^{(n)}=\lceil 1/{\varepsilon}\rceil+\lceil\log\ell^{(n+1)}/\log(3/2)\rceil
.

Lemma 8.7
.

For any
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}
and
m\geqslant 1
we have

\displaystyle a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)\leqslant{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right){\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n-1}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\min\left(\left({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{n}\right)^{-W^{n}/{\varepsilon}^{2}},e^{1/({\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha})}\right).

(68)

Proof of
8.5
.

From
8.6
and
8.7
and the explicit expressions
Eq.

40
, we get

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant e^{\log^{O(1)}(1/q)}\prod_{n=0}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1}\left({\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right){\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)\right)^{-O(1)}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant e^{\log^{O(1)}(1/q)}\prod_{n=0}^{N^{\mathrm{int}}-1}\left({\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right){\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)\right)^{-O(\log(1/q))}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log(1/q)\log\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right).

Since the second inequality in
8.5
is contained in
8.7
, this concludes the proof of the theorem modulo
8.7
and
8.6
.
∎

Proof of
8.6
.

Let us start by recalling a general fact about product measures. Consider two disjoint regions
A,B\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and a product measure
{\nu}
on
{\Omega}_{A}\times{\Omega}_{B}
. The law of total variance and convexity give
