\operatorname{Var}_{{\nu}_{A\cup B}}(f)={\nu}_{B}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{{\nu}_{A}}(f)\right)+\operatorname{Var}_{{\nu}_{B}}\left({\nu}_{A}(f)\right)\leqslant{\nu}(\operatorname{Var}_{{\nu}_{A}}(f)+\operatorname{Var}_{{\nu}_{B}}(f)).

(69)

Fix
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}}]
. Applying
Eq.

69
several times (in view of
8.2
and
8.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)

(70)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{D_{1}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1})\right)+\sum_{{\kappa}=2}^{K-1}\operatorname{Var}_{R_{\kappa}}\left(f|\mathcal{T}_{n}\left(R_{\kappa}\right)\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\sum_{{\kappa}=1}^{K-1}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{(n+1)}}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{D_{\kappa}\cup D_{1}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}),\mathcal{T}_{n}(D_{\kappa}\setminus D_{1})\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right),

where
R_{\kappa}=D_{\kappa}\setminus\bigcup_{{\kappa}^{\prime}=1}^{{\kappa}-1}D_{{\kappa}^{\prime}}
. Since the terms above are treated identically (except
{\kappa}=1
, which is actually simpler), without loss of generality we focus on
{\kappa}=2
.

Recall from
8.3
that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1})
was defined by East-extending
D_{0}
in direction
u_{1}
. Further East-extend
D_{0}
by
l_{2}
(recall that
D_{2}=y_{2}u_{1}+{\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(n)}+l_{2}\underline{v}_{0})
) in direction
u_{0}
, so that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2})
is also defined. Let
V=D_{1}\cup D_{2}
(that is a
\dashv
shaped region in
Fig.

14
) and

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V)=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1})\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}(D_{2}\setminus D_{1}).

(71)

Using a two-block dynamics (see e.g.
A.1
), we have

\operatorname{Var}_{V}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V))\\
\leqslant\frac{{\mu}_{V}(\operatorname{Var}_{D_{1}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}))+{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{E}}\operatorname{Var}_{V\setminus D_{1}}(f|\mathcal{T}_{n}(V\setminus D_{1}))|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V))}{{\Omega}({\mu}(\mathcal{E}|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V)))},

(72)

where

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}+y_{2}u_{1}\right)\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}\left(D_{1}\cap D_{2}\right)\subset{\Omega}_{D_{1}}.

(73)

Recalling
4.1
,
8.1
and
8.3
,
Eq.

73
and the fact that each segment of length
\ell^{(n)}/({\varepsilon}C)\gg{\delta}\ell^{(n)}/{\varepsilon}
intersects at most
O(1)
droplets, we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}(V\setminus D_{1})

\displaystyle{}\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}+y_{2}u_{1}\right)\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(D_{2}\setminus\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}+y_{2}u_{1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2}).

(74)

By
Eq.

74
and convexity of the variance, we obtain

{\mu}_{V}\left(\left.{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{E}}\operatorname{Var}_{V\setminus D_{1}}(f|\mathcal{T}_{n}(V\setminus D_{1}))\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V)\right)\\
\begin{aligned} &{}\leqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{E})}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V))}{\mu}_{V}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{D_{2}}\left(f|\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}\left(V\setminus D_{1}\right)\right)\right)\\
&{}\leqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{E}){\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2})){\mu}_{V}(\operatorname{Var}_{D_{2}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V)){\mu}(\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}(V\setminus D_{1}))}\\
&{}\leqslant\frac{{\mu}_{V}(\operatorname{Var}_{D_{2}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2})))}{{\mu}^{2}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}))}.\end{aligned}

(75)

Indeed, in the last line we recalled the definitions of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2})
,
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V)
and
\mathcal{E}
(see
4.4
,
73
and
71
), while in the second one we took into account that for any events
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}
with
{\mu}(\mathcal{A})>0
it holds that

\operatorname{Var}(f|\mathcal{A})=\min_{c\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mu}\left(\left.(f-c)^{2}\right|\mathcal{A}\right)\leqslant\frac{{\mu}((f-{\mu}(f|\mathcal{B}))^{2}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{A}})}{{\mu}(\mathcal{A})}\leqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{B})}{{\mu}(\mathcal{A})}\operatorname{Var}(f|\mathcal{B})

(76)

and
Eq.

74
.

We plug
Eq.

75
in
Eq.

72
and note that by the Harris inequality,
Eqs.

7
and
8
,
{\mu}(\mathcal{E}|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V))\geqslant{\mu}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)})){\mu}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}))
. This yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{V}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V)\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{O(1){\mu}_{V}(\operatorname{Var}_{D_{1}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}))+\operatorname{Var}_{D_{2}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)})){\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V)){\mu}^{3}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}))}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{O(1){\mu}_{V}(\operatorname{Var}_{D_{1}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}))+\operatorname{Var}_{D_{2}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2})))}{{\mu}^{2}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})){\mu}^{3}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}))}

(77)

where the second inequality uses
Eqs.

71
and
8.3
.

As in
Eqs.

43
and
44
,
4.6
gives

{\gamma}(D_{2})\leqslant\max\left({\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right),{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right)\right)e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}q^{-O(WM)}\\
\times\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{2}))}\prod_{m=1}^{M}a_{m}^{(n)}

(78)

with
M=\min\{m:{\lambda}_{0}(3/2)^{m+1}\geqslant l_{2}\}\leqslant M^{(n)}
. Plugging
Eqs.

78
and
15
(and their analogues for
D_{1}
) into
Eq.

77
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\gamma}(V)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(n)})e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)}}{{\mu}^{3}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})){\mu}^{3}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}))\min_{\kappa}{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{\kappa}))}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\frac{{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}\prod_{m=1}^{M^{(n)}}a_{m}^{(n)}}{{\mu}^{4}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right){\mu}^{4}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}))},

where the last inequality uses
Eq.

74
and that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1})\supset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})
by
8.3
. Plugging this into
Eq.

70
, concludes the proof of
8.6
, since
K=O(\ell^{(n+1)}/\ell^{(n)})\leqslant O(\log^{4}(1/q))
, as noted in
7.1
.
∎

Proof of
8.7
.

The first inequality in
Eq.

68
follows from the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
, while the second one is trivial. Therefore, we turn to the last one and fix
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}}]
. Note that by
4.4
,
8.1
and
8.3

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)\geqslant{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right){\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right){\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(D_{1}\setminus D_{0}\right)\right).

(79)

We therefore proceed by induction starting with

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(0)}\right)\right)=q^{|{\Lambda}^{(0)}|}=q^{\Theta(1/{\varepsilon}^{2})}.

(80)

We observe that from
8.1
, in order to ensure the occurrence of
\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{(n+1)})
, it suffices to have
O(WK\ell^{(n+1)})/(\ell^{(n)}{\delta})
well-placed
W
-helping sets and
O((\ell^{(n+1)})^{2})/(\ell^{(n)}{\delta}{\varepsilon})
helping sets for segments of length
{\delta}\ell^{(n)}/(3{\varepsilon})
. Indeed, we may split lines perpendicular to each
u_{j}
for
j\in(-k,k)
into successive disjoint segments of length
{\delta}\ell^{(n)}/(3{\varepsilon})
with a possible smaller leftover. It is then sufficient to place
W
-helping sets or helping sets depending on whether the segment under consideration is close to a parallel boundary of one of the
D_{\kappa}
or not. Note that here we crucially use the assumption that each segment of length
\ell^{(n)}/(C{\varepsilon})\gg{\delta}\ell^{(n)}/{\varepsilon}
intersects only
O(1)
droplets.

Recall that
1/{\varepsilon}\gg 1/{\delta}\gg W\gg 1
,
\ell^{(N^{\mathrm{cr}})}=W^{O(1)}q^{\alpha}
,
K=O(\ell^{(n+1)}/\ell^{(n)})\leqslant\log^{O(1)}(1/q)
, the explicit expressions
Eq.

40
and
3.11
. Then the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
, yields

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)\\
\begin{aligned} \geqslant{}&q^{O(W^{2}K\ell^{(n+1)})/(\ell^{(n)}{\delta})}\left(1-e^{-q^{\alpha}{\delta}\ell^{(n)}/O({\varepsilon})}\right)^{O((\ell^{(n+1)})^{2}/(\ell^{(n)}{\delta}{\varepsilon}))}\\
\geqslant{}&e^{-\log^{O(1)}(1/q)}\times\begin{cases}\left({\delta}q^{\alpha}W^{n}\right)^{W^{n}/({\delta}^{2}{\varepsilon})}&n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}}\\
\exp\left(-1/\left(q^{{\alpha}}\exp\left(W^{\exp(n-N^{\mathrm{cr}})}\right)\right)\right)&n>N^{\mathrm{cr}}.\end{cases}\end{aligned}

(81)

Essentially the same computation leads to the same bound for
{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}\setminus D_{0}))
(see
Eq.

59
). The only difference is that only
O(1)

W
-helping sets and
O(\ell^{(n+1)}/{\varepsilon})
helping sets are needed. Further recalling
Eqs.

80
and
79
, it is not hard to check
Eq.

68
.
∎

8.2
FA-1f global dynamics

We next import the global FA-1f dynamics together with much of the mesoscopic multi-directional East one simultaneously from
[
21
]
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
have a finite number of stable directions,
T=\exp(\log^{4}(1/q)/q^{\alpha})
and
\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}
be such that the associated side lengths satisfy
C\leqslant s^{\mathrm{int}}_{j}\leqslant O(\ell^{\mathrm{int}})
for all
j\in[4k]
. Assume that for all
l\in[0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}]
multiple of
{\lambda}_{0}
the event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+l\underline{v}_{0}))
is nonempty, decreasing, translation invariant and satisfies

\left(1-{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}\left(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+l\underline{v}_{0}\right)\right)\right)\right)^{T}T^{W}=o(1).

Then,

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mu}}[{\tau}_{0}]\leqslant\frac{\max_{l\in[0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}]}{\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+l\underline{v}_{0}))}{(q^{1/{\delta}}\min_{l\in[0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}]}{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+l\underline{v}_{0}))))^{\log(1/q)/{\delta}}}.

The proof is as in
[
21
]
, up to the following minor modifications. Firstly, one needs to replace the base of the snail by
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+{\lambda}_{0}\lceil\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}/{\lambda}_{0}\rceil\underline{v}_{0})
, which has a similar shape by hypothesis. Secondly, the event that the base is super good on
[
21
]
should be replaced by
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}})
. Finally,
[
21
]
*Proposition 4.9 is substituted by the definition
Eq.

15
of
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}})
. As
8.8
is essentially the entire content of
[
21
]
(see particularly Proposition 4.12 and Remark 4.8 there), we refer the reader to that work for the details.

Proof of
1
(e)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be balanced rooted with finite number of stable directions. Recall
{\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
with
\underline{r}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}=:\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}
from
Section

7.1
if
k=1
and from
Section

8.1
if
k\geqslant 2
. Fix
l\in[0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}]
multiple of
{\lambda}_{0}
and East-extend
{\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}
by
l
in direction
u_{0}
. It is not hard to check from
4.4
and
3.11
that

\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+l\underline{v}_{0})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}})))}={\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}},l,0\right)\right)\right)=q^{O(W)}

(see
Eq.

39
).
Then, by
4.6
,
8.5
and
7.3
and the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}\left(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+l\underline{v}_{0}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-2}{{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}\left(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{int}}+l\underline{v}_{0}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\begin{cases}\exp\left(\frac{\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right)&k=1,\\
\exp\left(\frac{2\log(1/q)\log\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right)&k\geqslant 2.\end{cases}

Plugging this in
8.8
, we obtain

{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mu}}[{\tau}_{0}]\leqslant\begin{cases}\exp\left(\frac{2\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right)&k=1,\\
\exp\left(\frac{3\log(1/q)\log\log\log(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right)&k\geqslant 2,\end{cases}

(82)

which concludes the proof of
1
(e)
in the case
k=1
and of
Eq.

4
for
k\geqslant 2
. The full result of
1
(e)
for
k\geqslant 2
is proved identically, replacing
8.5
by the stronger
C.1
.
∎

9
Balanced models with infinite number of stable directions

We finally turn to balanced models with infinite number of stable directions (class
(b)
). The internal dynamics was already handled in
Section

8.1
. The mesoscopic one (
Section

9.1
) is essentially the same as the the internal one, using two-dimensional East-extensions. The global dynamics (
Section

9.2
) also uses an East mechanism analogous to the FA-1f one from
[
21
]
used in
Section

8.2
.

9.1
East mesoscopic dynamics

Given that the bound we are aiming for in
1
(b)
is much larger than those in previous sections, there is a lot of margin and our reasoning is far from tight for the sake of simplicity.

Recall
N^{\mathrm{int}}
and
\ell^{(n)}
for
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{int}}
from
Eq.

40
, the droplets
{\Lambda}^{(n)}
from
Section

8.1
, their SG events from
8.4
. For
n>N^{\mathrm{int}}
, we set
\ell^{(n)}=W^{n-N^{\mathrm{int}}}\ell^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}
and define
\underline{s}^{(n)},\underline{r}^{(n)},{\Lambda}^{(n)}
as in
Section

8.1
. Recall
Section

3.4
. Further let
N^{\mathrm{mes}}=\inf\{n:\ell^{(n)}/{\varepsilon}\geqslant\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}=q^{-C}\}=\Theta(C\log(1/q)/\log W)
and assume for simplicity that
\ell^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}=q^{-C}{\varepsilon}
. We are only be interested in
n\leqslant N^{\mathrm{mes}}
and extend
8.1
,
8.4
and
8.3
to such
n
without change. With these conventions, our goal is the following.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a balanced model with infinitely many stable directions (class
(b)
). Then

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{3}q^{\alpha}}\right),

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}\right)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\exp\left(\frac{-2}{{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}}\right).

Proof of
9.1
.

The proof is essentially identical to the one of
8.5
, so we only indicate the necessary changes. To start with,
8.6
applies without change for
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}},N^{\mathrm{mes}})
. Also, the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
still implies that
a_{m}^{(n)}\leqslant{\mu}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(n)}))\leqslant{\mu}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}))
. Therefore,

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{m})}\right)\leqslant\frac{{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})e^{\log^{O(1)}(1/q)}}{({\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}))\min_{n\in[N^{\mathrm{mes}}]}{\mu}(\mathcal{T}_{n}({\Lambda}^{n+1})))^{O(N^{\mathrm{mes}}M^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}}-1)})}}.

Recalling the bound on
{\gamma}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
established in
8.5
, together with the fact that
N^{\mathrm{mes}}\leqslant C\log(1/q)
and
M^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}}-1)}\leqslant O(C\log(1/q))
, it suffices to prove that

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}\right)\right)\min_{n\in[N^{\mathrm{mes}}]}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n}\left({\Lambda}^{n+1}\right)\right)\geqslant\exp\left(-2/\left({\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}\right)\right),

(83)

in order to conclude the proof of
9.1
.

Once again, the proof of
Eq.

83
proceeds similarly to the one of
Eq.

68
in
8.7
. Indeed, the same computation as
Eq.

81
in the present setting gives that for
n\in[N^{\mathrm{int}},N^{\mathrm{mes}})
we have

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\right)\right)\geqslant q^{O(W^{3}/{\delta})}\exp\left(-e^{-q^{\alpha}{\delta}\ell^{(n)}/O({\varepsilon})}O\left(W^{2}\ell^{(n)}/({\delta}{\varepsilon})\right)\right)

(84)

and similarly for
{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}\setminus D_{0}))
(as in the proof of
8.7
, also see
Eq.

59
). From
Eq.

79
it follows that

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}\right)\right)\geqslant{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)\right)\\
\times\prod_{n=N^{\mathrm{int}}}^{N^{\mathrm{mes}}-1}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}\setminus D_{0})\right){\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}_{n}\left({\Lambda}^{(n+1)}\setminus{\Lambda}^{(n)}\right)\right).

Plugging
Eqs.

68
and
84
in the r.h.s., this yields
Eq.

83
as desired.
∎

9.2
East global dynamics

For the global dynamics we use a simpler version of the procedure of
[
21
]
*Section 5 with East dynamics instead of FA-1f.

Q_{i+1}
Q_{i}
Q_{i-1}
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}}
l=\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}})
{\Lambda}

Figure 15
:
Illustration of the East global dynamics (
Section

9.2
). The shaded droplet
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}}
inscribed in the box
Q
is extended by
2l
to the thickened one
{\Lambda}
.

Proof of
1
(b)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be balanced with infinite number of stable directions and recall
Section

9.1
. Set
T=\exp(1/q^{3{\alpha}})
,
\underline{s}^{\mathrm{mes}}=\underline{s}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}
,
\underline{r}^{\mathrm{mes}}=\underline{r}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}
and
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}}={\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{mes}})}
. In particular,
s^{\mathrm{mes}}_{j}=\Theta(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}})
for
j\in[-k,k+1]
and
s^{\mathrm{mes}}_{j}=\Theta(1/{\varepsilon})
for
j\in[k+2,3k-1]
. We East-extend
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}}
by
2l=2({\lambda}_{0}+r^{\mathrm{mes}}_{0}+r^{\mathrm{mes}}_{2k})
in direction
u_{0}
to obtain
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{mes}}+2l\underline{v}_{0})
.
4.6
,
9.1
and
4.4
, the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
and the simple fact that
{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T(\underline{r}^{\mathrm{mes}},2l,0)))=q^{O(W)}
(by
3.11
and
4.2
as usual) give

\displaystyle{\gamma}({\Lambda})\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/q)}{{\varepsilon}^{O(1)}q^{\alpha}}\right),

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)\geqslant{}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{-3}{{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}}\right).

(85)

A similar argument to the rest of the proof was already discussed thoroughly in
[
21
]
*Section 5 and then in
[
23
]
*Section 5, so we only provide a sketch. The adapted approach of
[
21
]
*Section 5 proceeds as follows.

(1)

Denoting
t_{*}=\exp(-1/({\varepsilon}^{W}q^{2{\alpha}}))
, by the main result of
[
33
]
it suffices to show that
T{\mathbb{P}}_{{\mu}}({\tau}_{0}>t_{*})=o(1)
, in order to deduce
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mu}}[{\tau}_{0}]\leqslant t_{*}+o(1)
.

(2)

By finite speed of propagation we may work with the
\mathcal{U}
-KCM on a large discrete torus of size
T\gg t_{*}
.

(3)

We partition the torus into strips and the strips into translates of the box
Q={\mathbb{H}}_{u_{0}}({\lambda}_{0}+r^{\mathrm{mes}}_{0})\cap{\mathbb{H}}_{u_{k}}({\rho}_{k}+r^{\mathrm{mes}}_{k})\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{-k}}(r^{\mathrm{mes}}_{-k})\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{2k}}(r^{\mathrm{mes}}_{2k})
as shown in
Fig.

15
. We say
Q
is
good
(
\mathcal{G}(Q)
occurs) if for each segment
S\subset Q
perpendicular to some
u\in\widehat{}\mathcal{S}
of length
{\varepsilon}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}
the event
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
occurs. Further define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}(Q)
to occur if the only (integer) translate of
{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}}
contained in
Q
is SG. We say that
the environment is good
(
\mathcal{E}
occurs) if all boxes are good and in each strip at least one box is super good. The sizes are chosen so that it is sufficiently likely for this event to always occur up to time
t_{*}
. Indeed, we have
(1-{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{\mathrm{mes}})))^{T}T^{W}=o(1)
by
9.1
and
(1-{\mu}_{Q}(\mathcal{G}))T^{W}=o(1)
by
3.11
.

(4)

By a standard variational technique it then suffices to prove a Poincaré inequality, bounding the variance of a function conditionally on
\mathcal{E}
by the Dirichlet form on the torus. Moreover, since
{\mu}
and
\mathcal{E}
are product w.r.t. the partition of
Fig.

15
, it suffices to prove this inequality on a single strip.

(5)

Finally, we prove such a bound, using an auxiliary East dynamics for the boxes and the definition of
{\gamma}
to reproduce the resampling of the state of a box by moves of the original
\mathcal{U}
-KCM.

Let us explain the last step above in more detail, as it is the only one that genuinely differs from
[
21
]
.

Let
Q_{i}=Q+ilu_{0}
and
{\mathbb{T}}=\bigcup_{i\in[T]}Q_{i}
be our strip of interest (indices are considered modulo
T
, since the strip is on the torus). As explained above, our goal is to prove that for all
f:{\Omega}_{\mathbb{T}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
it holds that

\operatorname{Var}_{{\mathbb{T}}}(f|\mathcal{E})\leqslant\exp\left(1/\left({\varepsilon}^{O(1)}q^{2{\alpha}}\right)\right)\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\mu}_{{\mathbb{T}}}\left(c_{x}^{{\mathbb{T}},\mathbf{1}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right),

(86)

where
c_{x}^{{\mathbb{T}},\mathbf{1}}
takes into account the periodic geometry of
{\mathbb{T}}
.

By
[
33
]
*Proposition 3.4 on the generalised East chain we have

\operatorname{Var}_{\mathbb{T}}(f|\mathcal{E})\leqslant\exp\left(1/\left({\varepsilon}^{5}q^{2{\alpha}}\right)\right)\sum_{i\in[T]}{\mu}_{{\mathbb{T}}}\left(\left.{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}(Q_{i-1})}\operatorname{Var}_{Q_{i}}\left(f|\mathcal{G}\left(Q_{i}\right)\right)\right|\mathcal{E}\right),

(87)

since
9.1
and the Harris inequality
Eq.

8
give
{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}(Q)|\mathcal{G}(Q))\geqslant\exp(-2/({\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}))
.
8
8

8

Strictly speaking
[
33
]
does not deal with the torus conditioned on having an infection, but this issue is easily dealt with by the method of
[
6
]
.

Next observe that
{\Lambda}_{i}\supset Q_{i}
, where
{\Lambda}_{i}={\Lambda}+(i-1)lu_{0}
(see
Fig.

15
). Let
\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}_{i}\setminus Q_{i})\subset\mathcal{G}(Q_{i+1})\cap\mathcal{G}(Q_{i-1})
be the event that
\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)
holds for all segments
S\subset{\Lambda}_{i}\setminus Q_{i}
of length
2{\varepsilon}\ell^{\mathrm{mes}}
perpendicular to some
u\in\widehat{}\mathcal{S}
. Hence, by convexity of the variance and the fact that
{\mu}(\mathcal{E})=1-o(1)
we have

{\mu}_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\left.{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}(Q_{i-1})}\operatorname{Var}_{Q_{i}}(f|\mathcal{G}(Q_{i}))\right|\mathcal{E}\right)\\
\begin{aligned} \leqslant{}&(1+o(1)){\mu}_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}_{i}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}(Q_{i-1})\cap\mathcal{G}(Q_{i})\cap\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}_{i}\setminus Q_{i}))\right),\\
\leqslant{}&(1+o(1)){\mu}_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}_{i}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{i})\right)\right).\end{aligned}

Here we used
Eq.

76
and
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}(Q_{i-1})\cap\mathcal{G}(Q_{i})\cap\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}_{i}\setminus Q_{i})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}_{i})
(recall
4.4
) for the second inequality. Finally, recalling
Eqs.

15
,
85
and
87
, we obtain
Eq.

86
as desired.
∎

As already noted, all lower bounds in
1
are known from
[
20
]
and the upper ones for classes
(a)
and
(c)
were proved in
[
33
]
and
[
21
]
respectively. Thus, the proof of
1
is complete.

Appendix A
Extensions

This appendix aims to prove our main building blocks—
4.6
and
4.9
for the East- and CBSEP-extensions.

A.1
Auxiliary two-block chain

We begin with a non-product variant of the standard two-block technique for the purposes of the proof of the East-extension
4.6
. Let
({\Omega}_{1},\pi_{1})
and
({\Omega}_{2},\pi_{2})
be finite positive probability spaces,
({\Omega},\pi)
denote the associated product space and
{\nu}={\pi}(\cdot|\mathcal{H})
for some event
\mathcal{H}\subset{\Omega}
. For
\omega\in{\Omega}
we write
{\omega}_{i}\in{\Omega}_{i}
for its
i
th
coordinate. Consider an event
\mathcal{F}\subset{\Omega}_{1}
and set

\mathcal{D}(f)={\nu}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{\nu}(f|{\omega}_{2})+{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{F}}\operatorname{Var}_{\nu}(f|{\omega}_{1})\right)

for any
f:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
. Observe that
\mathcal{D}
is the Dirichlet form of the continuous time Markov chain on
\mathcal{H}
in which
{\omega}_{1}
is resampled at rate one from

{\nu}(\cdot|{\omega}_{2})
and, if
{\omega}_{1}\in\mathcal{F}
, then
{\omega}_{2}
is resampled with rate one from

{\nu}(\cdot|{\omega}_{1})
. This chain is
reversible w.r.t.
{\nu}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Assume that
\mathcal{F}\times{\Omega}_{2}\subset\mathcal{H}
. Then, for all
f:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\operatorname{Var}_{\nu}(f)\leqslant O(1)\max_{{\omega}_{2}\in{\Omega}_{2}}{\nu}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}|{\omega}_{2})\mathcal{D}(f).

Proof.

We follow
[
23
]
*Proposition 3.5. Consider the Markov chain
({\omega}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
described above. Given two arbitrary initial conditions
{\omega}(0)
an
{\omega}^{\prime}(0)
we construct a coupling of two of such chains with these initial conditions such that with probability
{\Omega}(1)
we have
{\omega}(t)={\omega}^{\prime}(t)
for
t>T=\max_{{\omega}_{2}\in{\Omega}_{2}}{\nu}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}|{\omega}_{2})
. Standard arguments
[
29
]
then prove that the mixing time of the chain is
O(T)
and the lemma follows.

To construct our coupling, we use the following representation of the Markov chain. We are given two independent Poisson clocks with rate one and the chain transitions occur only at the clock rings. When the first clock rings, a Bernoulli variable

\xi
with probability of success
{\nu}(\mathcal{F}|{\omega}_{2})

is sampled. If

\xi=1
, then
{\omega}_{1}
is resampled w.r.t. the measure
\pi(\cdot|\mathcal{F})={\nu}(\cdot|\mathcal{F},{\omega}_{2})
, while if
\xi=0
, then
{\omega}_{1}
is
resampled w.r.t. the measure
{\nu}(\cdot|\mathcal{F}^{c},{\omega}_{2})
. Clearly, in doing so

{\omega}_{1}
is resampled w.r.t.
{\nu}(\cdot|{\omega}_{2})
. If the second clock rings,
we resample
{\omega}_{2}
from
{\pi}_{2}
if
{\omega}_{1}\in\mathcal{F}
and ignore the ring otherwise.

Both chains use the same clocks. When the first clock rings and the current couple of configurations is

({\omega},{\omega}^{\prime})
, we first maximally couple the two Bernoulli variables
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
corresponding to
{\omega},{\omega}^{\prime}
respectively. Then:

•

if
\xi=\xi^{\prime}=1
, we update both
{\omega}_{1}
and
{\omega}^{\prime}_{1}
to the
same

\eta_{1}\in\mathcal{F}
with probability
\pi(\eta_{1}|\mathcal{F})
;

•

otherwise, we resample
{\omega}_{1}
and
{\omega}^{\prime}_{1}
independently from their respective laws, given
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
.

When the second clock rings, the two chains attempt to update to two maximally coupled configurations with the corresponding distributions.

Suppose now that two consecutive rings occur at times
t_{1}<t_{2}
at the first and second clocks respectively and the Bernoulli variables at time
t_{1}
are both
1
. Then the two configurations are clearly identical at
t_{2}
. To conclude the proof, observe that for any time interval
{\Delta}
of length one the probability that there exist
t_{1}<t_{2}
in
{\Delta}
as above is at least
1/(4T)
.
∎

A.2
Microscopic dynamics

We next turn to the microscopic dynamics (recall
Section

2.2
).

Recall
3.7
. Let
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet with side lengths at least
C^{3}
. Given
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}}
and
i\in[4k]
, we define
{\Lambda}\subset{\Lambda}_{i}^{\omega}\subset{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+O(1)\underline{v}_{i})
by
{\Lambda}_{i}^{\omega}={\Lambda}
if
{\alpha}(u_{i})=0
or
{\alpha}(u_{i})>{\alpha}
. If
{\alpha}(u_{i})\in(0,{\alpha}]
, we rather set

{\Lambda}_{i}^{\omega}={\Lambda}\cup\bigcup_{x}\left(\left(\left[Z_{i}\cup{\mathbb{H}}_{u_{i}}\right]_{\mathcal{U}}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{i}}\right)+x\right)\setminus\left\{y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}:{\omega}_{y}=0\right\},

the union being over
x\in{\Lambda}
such that
{\omega}_{(x+Z_{i})\setminus{\Lambda}}=\mathbf{0}
and
x
is at distance at least
C
from all sides of
{\Lambda}
except the
u_{i}
-side. In words, we essentially look at pieces of
u_{i}
-helping sets for the last few lines of the droplet sticking out of it and add to
{\Lambda}
the sites which each piece can infect. The reason for introducing this is that helping sets may need to infect a few sites outside
{\Lambda}
before creating their periodic infections on the corresponding line and it is those sites that we wish to include in
{\Lambda}_{i}^{\omega}
. We set
{\Lambda}_{I}^{\omega}=\bigcup_{i\in I}{\Lambda}_{i}^{\omega}
for
I\subset[4k]
.
Let
i\in[4k]
be such that
{\alpha}(u_{j})<\infty
for all
j\in I=\{i-k+1,\dots,i+k-1\}
. Fix
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}(\underline{r})
with side lengths at least
C^{3}
and at most
q^{-O(C)}
. Let
l\in[0,O(1)]
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
,
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})}
,
{\Lambda}^{+}=({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))_{I}^{\omega}
and
T=T(\underline{r},l,i)
. Our goal is to provide a relaxation mechanism for an East-extension of bounded length.

Lemma A.2
.

In the above setting we have

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{T}\left(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T)\right)\right)\leqslant e^{O(\log^{2}(1/q))}\sum_{x\in{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}}\left(c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda},\mathbf{0}_{\Lambda}\cdot{\omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{+}}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right)

(88)

and the same holds for
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}
instead of
\mathcal{T}
.

Though it is possible to prove this directly via canonical paths, we rather deduce it from the main result of
[
26
]
proved much more elegantly. That work was developed for the purpose of its present application.

Proof.

We only treat
\mathcal{T}
, the proof for
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}
being identical. Let us denote by
\mathcal{E}^{\omega}
the event that the
\mathcal{U}
-KCM restricted to
{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}
with boundary condition
\mathbf{0}_{\Lambda}\cdot{\omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{+}}
is able to fully infect
{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}
. As in the proof of
4.5
, we see that
\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T)\subset\mathcal{E}^{\omega}
. Moreover, recalling
4.2
, we have
{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T))\geqslant{\mu}(\mathcal{W}(T))\geqslant q^{O(W)}
, since
T
has bounded length. Hence, by
Eqs.

69
and
76
,

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{T}\left(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T)\right)\right)\leqslant\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}}\left(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T)\right)\leqslant q^{-O(W)}\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}}\left(f|\mathcal{E}^{\omega}\right).

We next observe that the process defining
\mathcal{E}^{\omega}
is in fact a one-dimensional inhomogeneous KCM of the type considered in
[
26
]
and called general KCM there (enumerate the sites of
{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}
so that neighbouring sites remain at bounded distance, e.g. in lexicographical order for
(\langle\cdot,u_{i+k}\rangle,\langle\cdot,u_{i}\rangle)
). Therefore,
[
26
]
*Theorem 1 yields
Eq.

88
as desired, taking into account that
{\mu}(\mathcal{E}^{\omega})\geqslant{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T))\geqslant q^{O(W)}
.
∎

Corollary A.3
.

In the same setting as above, we have

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{T}(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T))\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)\\
\leqslant e^{O(\log^{2}(1/q))}\max\left({\gamma}({\Lambda}),{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)\right)\sum_{x\in{\Lambda}^{+}}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{+},{\omega}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right)

(89)

and the same holds with
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}
instead of
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

By
A.2
, it suffices to bound

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(\left.c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda},\mathbf{0}_{\Lambda}\cdot{\omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{+}}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)

from above by the r.h.s. of
Eq.

89
for any
x\in{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}
. By
Eq.

69
this is at most

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda},\mathbf{0}_{\Lambda}\cdot{\omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{+}}}\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}\cup\{x\}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)\right).

By the two-block
A.1
we have

\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}\cup\{x\}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)\\
\leqslant q^{-O(1)}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}\cup\{x\}}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{\Lambda}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)+{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right),

where
\mathcal{I}
is the event that all sites in
{\Lambda}
at distance at most some large constant from
x
are infected. Putting this together and observing that

{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda},\mathbf{0}_{\Lambda}\cdot{\omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{+}}}\leqslant c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{+},{\omega}}
, we get

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}(\operatorname{Var}_{T}(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T))|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}))\leqslant e^{O(\log^{2}(1/q))}\times\\
\left(|{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}|{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{\Lambda}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)\right)+\sum_{x\in{\Lambda}^{+}\setminus{\Lambda}}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(\left.c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{+},{\omega}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\right)\right).

Finally, recalling
Eq.

15
and
|{\Lambda}^{+}|\leqslant q^{-O(C)}
, we recover
Eq.

89
.
∎

A.3
Proofs of the one-directional extensions

We require a more technical version of
Eq.

15
accounting for a boundary condition. For a droplet
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}(\underline{r})
, boundary condition
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}}
, nonempty event
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda})\subset{\Omega}_{\Lambda}
and set of directions
I\subset[4k]
, let
{\gamma}_{I}^{\omega}({\Lambda})
be the smallest constant
{\gamma}\in[1,\infty]
such that

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{\omega}_{I}}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}\right)\right)\right)\leqslant{\gamma}\sum_{x\in{\Lambda}_{I}^{\omega}}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{\omega}_{I}}\left(c_{x}^{{\Lambda}^{\omega}_{I},{\omega}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right).

(90)

holds for all
f:{\Omega}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
.

For the rest of the section we recall the following notation from
4.6
. Let
i\in[4k]
be such that
{\alpha}(u_{j})<\infty
for all
j\in(i-k,i+k)
. Let
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}(\underline{r})
be a droplet with
\underline{r}=q^{-O(C)}
and side lengths at least
C^{3}
. Let
l\in(0,\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}]
be a multiple of
{\lambda}_{i}
. Let
d_{m}={\lambda}_{i}\lfloor(3/2)^{m}\rfloor
for
m\in[1,M)
and
M=\min\{m:{\lambda}_{i}(3/2)^{m}\geqslant l\}
. Let
d_{M}=l
,
{\Lambda}^{m}={\Lambda}(\underline{r}+d_{m}\underline{v}_{i})
for
m\in[1,M]
and
s_{m-1}=d_{m}-d_{m-1}
for
m\in[2,M]
.

Lemma A.4
.

Set
I=\{i-k+1,\dots,i+k-1\}
. Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
be a nonempty translation invariant decreasing event. Assume that we East-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
. Then

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{M}\right)\leqslant\max_{{\omega}}{\gamma}_{I}^{{\omega}}\left({\Lambda}^{1}\right)\prod_{m=1}^{M-1}\frac{a_{m}}{q^{O(W)}}

where
a_{m}
is defined in
Eq.

17
.

Proof.

We loosely follow
[
23
]
*Eq. (4.10). Note that by
Eqs.

15
and
90

{\gamma}_{I}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{M})={\gamma}({\Lambda}^{M})
. Proceeding by induction it then suffices to prove that for any
m\in[1,M)
and
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{m+1}}

{\gamma}_{I}^{{\omega}}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\leqslant\max_{{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{m}}}{\gamma}_{I}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}\left({\Lambda}^{m}\right)\frac{a_{m}}{q^{O(W)}}.

(91)

Fix such
m
and
{\omega}
and partition
{\Lambda}^{m+1}=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup V_{3}
so that

\displaystyle V_{1}\cup V_{2}

\displaystyle{}={\Lambda}^{m},

\displaystyle V_{2}\cup V_{3}

\displaystyle{}={\Lambda}^{m}+s_{m}u_{i}.

That is, set
V_{1}=s_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},s_{m},i+2k)
,
V_{2}=s_{m}+{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+(d_{m}-s_{m})\underline{v}_{i})
and
V_{3}=d_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},s_{m},i)
.

In order to apply
A.1
, we define
{\Omega}_{1}={\Omega}_{{\Lambda}^{m}}
,
{\Omega}_{2}=\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})
and equip them with
{\pi}_{1}={\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{m}}
and
{\pi}_{2}={\mu}_{V_{3}}(\cdot|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3}))
respectively. We set
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}^{m+1})
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{m})\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})
. Note that these these
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}
events were defined when East-extending
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
, since
0\leqslant d_{m}-s_{m}\leqslant d_{m}\leqslant d_{m+1}\leqslant d_{M}=l
(for
V_{2}
we also use translation invariance). Notice that
\mathcal{F}\times{\Omega}_{2}\subset\mathcal{H}
, since, by
4.4
,
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{m})=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T(\underline{r},d_{m},i))
and

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T(\underline{r},d_{m},i))\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T(\underline{r},d_{m+1},i))

by
4.3
. We may therefore apply
A.1
to get

\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m+1}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\right)\leqslant O(1)\max_{\eta_{V_{3}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}^{m+1}),\eta_{V_{3}}\right)\\
\times{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{m+1}}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}}(f|\mathcal{H},\eta_{V_{3}})+{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{F}}\operatorname{Var}_{V_{3}}\left(f|\mathcal{H},\eta_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\right).

(92)

Note that by
4.4
for any
\eta_{V_{3}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})
we have

\eta_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\cdot\eta_{V_{3}}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\Leftrightarrow\eta_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\eta_{V_{3}}\cdot{\omega}}({\Lambda}^{m}),

which implies that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\left(f|\mathcal{H},\eta_{V_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\eta}_{V_{3}}\cdot{\omega}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right)

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{F}|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right),{\eta}_{V_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle{}={\mu}\left(\mathcal{F}|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\eta}_{V_{3}}\cdot{\omega}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right).

Further note that by
4.4
and
4.1
,

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(s_{m}u_{i}+{\Lambda}(\underline{r})\right)\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(s_{m}u_{i}+T\left(\underline{r},d_{m}-s_{m},i\right)\right)\\
\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\cap\mathcal{T}^{{\eta}_{s_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},d_{m}-s_{m},i)}\cdot\mathbf{1}}\left(T\left(\underline{r},s_{m},i\right)\right),

the second SG event being implied by
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\eta_{V_{3}}\cdot{\omega}}({\Lambda}^{m})
again by
4.4
. Applying
4.2
and
9
, we get that for any
{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}^{m}}

\displaystyle{}{\mu}(\mathcal{F}|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m}))

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\begin{multlined}{\mu}\Big(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{T}^{{\eta}_{s_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},d_{m}-s_{m},i)}\cdot\mathbf{1}}\left(T(\underline{r},s_{m},i)\right)\\
\cap\mathcal{W}\left(T\left(\underline{r},s_{m},i\right)\right)\big|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m})\Big)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle{}={\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}\left(T(\underline{r},s_{m},i)\right)\cap\mathcal{W}\left(T(\underline{r},s_{m},i)\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right)

\displaystyle{}={\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{W}\left(T(\underline{r},s_{m},i)\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant q^{O(W)}{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right),

where in the second inequality we used that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}(T(\underline{r},s_{m},i))\supset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m})
, using
4.4
and
s_{m}\leqslant d_{m}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{F}\times{\Omega}_{2}\subset\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})
, we have

{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{F}}\operatorname{Var}_{V_{3}}\left(f|\mathcal{H},\eta_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\right)\leqslant{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})}\operatorname{Var}_{V_{3}}\left(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})\right).

Plugging the above back into
Eq.

92
yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m+1}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\right)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle q^{-O(W)}\max_{{\omega}^{\prime}}{\mu}^{-1}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right)

(93)

\displaystyle\times{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{m+1}}\big(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\eta}_{V_{3}}\cdot{\omega}}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})}\operatorname{Var}_{V_{3}}\left(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})\right)\big|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\big).

From
Eq.

90
we have

{\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{(m+1)})_{I}^{\omega}}\left(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\eta}_{V_{3}}\cdot{\omega}}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\right)\right)\\
\leqslant\max_{{\omega}^{\prime}}{\gamma}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{I}\left({\Lambda}^{m}\right)\sum_{x\in({\Lambda}^{m+1})^{\omega}_{I}}{\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega}}\left(c_{x}^{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega},{\omega}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right).

On the other hand, recalling by
4.4
that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}^{m+1})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})
,

{\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{(m+1)})_{I}^{\omega}}\left(\left.{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}(V_{2})}\operatorname{Var}_{V_{3}}(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3}))\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\right)\\
\begin{aligned} \leqslant{}&\frac{{\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega}}({\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})}\operatorname{Var}_{V_{3}}(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})))}{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}^{m+1})}\\
={}&\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3}))}{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}({\Lambda}^{m+1})}{\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega}}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{V_{3}}(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3}))\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})\right)\\
\leqslant{}&\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}(s_{m}u_{i}+{\Lambda}^{m})){\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega}}(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}+s_{m}u_{i}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))){\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T(\underline{r},s_{m},i))){\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(s_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},d_{m},i)))}\\
={}&\frac{{\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega}}(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}+s_{m}u_{i}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(V_{3})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{3}))}\\
\leqslant{}&\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(s_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},d_{m},i))){\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega}}(\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{m}+s_{m}u_{i}}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}(s_{m}u_{i}+{\Lambda}^{m})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(s_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},d_{m}-s_{m},i))){\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(d_{m}u_{i}+T(\underline{r},s_{m},i))){\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{3}))}\\
\leqslant{}&\frac{{\gamma}^{\omega}_{I}({\Lambda}^{m})}{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{3}))q^{O(W)}}\sum_{x\in({\Lambda}^{m}+s_{m}u_{i})_{I}^{\omega}}{\mu}_{({\Lambda}^{m+1})_{I}^{\omega}}\left(c_{x}^{({\Lambda}^{m}+s_{m}u_{i})^{\omega}_{I},{\omega}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right),\end{aligned}

where we used
4.4
,
4.3
and
69
in the second inequality; translation invariance and
4.4
in the second equality;
Eqs.

76
,
4.4
and
4.3
in the third inequality; and
4.3
,
4.2
and
90
in the last one. Plugging these bounds into
Eq.

93
, we obtain

{\gamma}^{\omega}_{I}\left({\Lambda}^{m+1}\right)\leqslant\frac{\max_{{\omega}^{\prime}}{\gamma}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{I}({\Lambda}^{m})}{q^{O(W)}{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{3}))\min_{{\omega}^{\prime}}{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m}))}.

It remains to transform the denominator in the last expression, fixing some
{\omega}^{\prime}
. Note that

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{3})\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}\left({\Lambda}^{m}\right)\right)\\
\begin{aligned} \geqslant{}&{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{3})\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\cap\mathcal{W}\left(s_{m}u_{i}+T\left(\underline{r},d_{m}-s_{m},i\right)\right)\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{m}\right)\right),\\
\geqslant{}&{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(s_{m}u_{i}+{\Lambda}^{m}\right)\cap\mathcal{W}\left(s_{m}u_{i}+T\left(\underline{r},d_{m}-s_{m},i\right)\right)\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{m}\right)\right)\\
\geqslant{}&q^{O(W)}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left(s_{m}u_{i}+{\Lambda}^{m}\right)\cap\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}^{m}\right)\right)\end{aligned}

using that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}
is decreasing in the boundary condition, then
4.3
,
4.2
and
4.4
and finally
4.2
and the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
. Moreover, by
4.4
and
4.2
,

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right)\leqslant q^{-O(W)}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{m})\right),

so that we recover

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{3})\right){\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}(V_{2})\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}\left({\Lambda}^{m}\right)\right)\geqslant q^{O(W)}/a_{m}

completing the proof of
Eq.

91
and
A.4
.
∎

Proof of
4.6
.

The fact that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
is nonempty, translation invariant and decreasing follows directly from
4.4
. By
A.4
it suffices to relate
\max_{{\omega}}{\gamma}^{\omega}_{I}({\Lambda}^{1})
and
{\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
, using
A.3
. Notice that by
4.4
we have

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{1}\right)=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\times\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}}(T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)).

(94)

Therefore, (see e.g.
[
21
]
*Lemma 3.9 or
Eq.

69
)

\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}^{1}}\left(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{1}\right)\right)\leqslant{\mu}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)}\left(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}\left(T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)\right)\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}\left(\underline{r}\right)\right)\right)\\
+{\mu}_{T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}(\underline{r})\right)\right|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}\left(T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)\right)\right).

(95)

The former term is treated by
A.3
, which gives

{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)}\left(f|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}\left(T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)\right)\right)\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}\left(\underline{r}\right)\right)\right)\\
\leqslant e^{O(\log^{2}(1/q))}\max\left({\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r})),{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\right)\right)\sum_{x\in{\Lambda}^{+}}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}^{+}}\left(c^{{\Lambda}^{+},{\omega}}_{x}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right),

where
{\Lambda}^{+}=({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+{\lambda}_{i}\underline{v}_{i})_{I}^{\omega})
. For the second term in
Eq.

95
,
Eq.

15
and
{\mu}_{T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)}(\mathcal{T}^{\omega}(T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)))\geqslant q^{O(W)}
(see the proof of
4.2
) give

{\mu}_{T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)}\left(\left.\operatorname{Var}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}(f|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}\left({\Lambda}(\underline{r})\right)\right|\mathcal{T}^{\omega}\left(T(\underline{r},{\lambda}_{i},i)\right)\right)\\
\leqslant q^{-O(W)}{\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\sum_{x\in{\Lambda}(\underline{r})}{\mu}_{{\Lambda}(\underline{r}+{\lambda}_{i}\underline{v}_{i})}\left(c_{x}^{{\Lambda}(\underline{r}),\mathbf{1}}\operatorname{Var}_{x}(f)\right).

Plugging these into
Eq.

95
and recalling
Eq.

90
, we get

{\gamma}^{\omega}\left({\Lambda}^{1}\right)\leqslant e^{O(\log^{2}(1/q))}\max\left({\gamma}({\Lambda}(\underline{r})),{\mu}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))\right)\right),

which concludes the proof of
4.6
together with
A.4
, since
M=O(\log(\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}))\leqslant O(C)\log(1/q)
.
∎

We next turn to CBSEP-extensions.

Lemma A.5
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}
has a finite number of stable directions. Set
J=[4k]\setminus\{i+k,i-k\}
. Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}))
be a nonempty translation invariant decreasing event. Assume we CBSEP-extend
{\Lambda}(\underline{r})
by
l
in direction
u_{i}
. Then

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})\right)\leqslant\max_{{\omega}}{\gamma}_{J}^{{\omega}}\left({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+{\lambda}_{i}\underline{v}_{i})\right)\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+{\lambda}_{i}\underline{v}_{i})))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i})))}e^{O(C^{2})\log^{2}(1/q)}.

Proof.

As in
[
23
]
*Eq. (4.10) (with minor amendments as in
A.4
), we have

{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{M}\right)\leqslant\max_{{\omega}}{\gamma}_{J}^{{\omega}}\left({\Lambda}^{1}\right)\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{1}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{M}))q^{O(MW)}}\prod_{m=1}^{M-1}b_{m}

(96)

with

b_{m}=\max_{\omega}{\mu}^{-2}_{{\Lambda}^{m+1}}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{s_{m}}\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\right)\max_{\omega}{\mu}^{-1}_{{\Lambda}^{m+1}}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\omega}\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}\right).

Let us mention that the only difference of
Eq.

96
w.r.t.
[
23
]
is the fraction in the r.h.s. It comes from the absence of the conditioning in the r.h.s. of
Eq.

15
as compared to
[
23
]
*Eq. (4.5) pointed out in
3.12
. This leads to
[
23
]
*Eq. (4.16) being slightly simpler in our setting. Namely, there one should use the fact that for any finite
A\subset B\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,
\mathcal{A}\subset{\Omega}_{A}
,
\mathcal{B}\subset{\Omega}_{B}
and
f:{\Omega}_{B}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
we have

{\mu}_{B}({\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mu}_{A}(f)|\mathcal{B})=\frac{{\mu}_{B}({\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{B}}{\mu}_{A}(f))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{B})}\leqslant\frac{{\mu}_{B}({\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mu}_{A}(f))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{B})}=\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{A})}{{\mu}(\mathcal{B})}{\mu}_{B}(f).

(97)

Using this yields

\prod_{m=1}^{M-1}\frac{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{m})}{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{0}}({\Lambda}^{m+1})}\leqslant\prod_{m=1}^{M-1}\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{m}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{m+1}))q^{O(W)}}=\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{1}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{M}))q^{O(MW)}},

using
4.2
. Up to this modification the proof is the same as in
[
23
]
, so we do not repeat it.

Given
Eq.

96
, we are left with proving
b_{m}\leqslant q^{-O(C)}
for all
m
. The last statement is simply
4.10
—the analogue of
[
23
]
*Corollary A.3, so we are done.
∎

Proof of
4.9
.

The fact that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}({\Lambda}(\underline{r}+l\underline{v}_{i}))
is nonempty, translation invariant and decreasing follows directly from
4.4
. By
A.5
it suffices to relate
{\gamma}_{J}^{\omega}({\Lambda}^{1})
and
{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}(\underline{r})\right)
. This is done exactly as in
[
23
]
*Lemma 4.10 (see particularly Eqs. (4.20) and (4.22) there), replacing
[
23
]
*Eq. (4.30) by
A.3
and using
Eqs.

97
and
4.2
as in the proof of
4.6
.

∎

Appendix B
Conditional probabilities

The goal of this appendix is to prove
4.10
and
4.11
. Recall
Section

4.4
and
4.7
.

Proof of
4.10
.

We prove that for all
s,s^{\prime}\in[0,l]
divisible by
{\lambda}_{i}
and
{\omega},{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus{\Lambda}}
we have

\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{s}({\Lambda}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}({\Lambda}))}=q^{O(W)}.

(98)

Once this is established, we note that by
4.7
,

\displaystyle\max_{s^{\prime}}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}({\Lambda})\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda})\right)={\mu}\left(\bigcup_{s^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}({\Lambda})\right)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant O(l)\max_{s^{\prime}}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}({\Lambda})\right).

Further recalling from
Section

3.4
, that
l\leqslant\ell^{\mathrm{mes}+}\leqslant q^{-O(C)}
and
W\ll C
, we get

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{s}({\Lambda})\right|\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda})\right)\geqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{s}({\Lambda}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda}))}\geqslant q^{-O(C)},

since
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{s}({\Lambda})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda})\subset\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}({\Lambda})
. Thus, it remains to prove
Eq.

98
. Moreover, it clearly suffices to establish
Eq.

98
for
s^{\prime}=0
and
{\omega}^{\prime}=\mathbf{1}
.

To prove
Eq.

98
in that case, let us first observe that by translation invariance,
4.7
and
16
,

\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\omega}_{s}({\Lambda}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}_{0}({\Lambda}))}=\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}_{s}}(T_{s})){\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}_{l-s}}(T_{l-s}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(T_{l}))},

(99)

where for
x\in\{s,l-s,l\}
,
T_{x}=T(\underline{r},x,i)
and the
{\omega}_{x}
is a boundary condition that can be expressed in terms of
{\omega}
and
x
. Applying
4.2
and
4.3
to
Eq.

99
, we obtain
Eq.

98
as desired.
∎

Our next goal is to treat certain perturbations of traversability events. To do that we not only require the Harris inequality but also the van den Berg–Kesten
[
38
]
one. We should note that the BK inequality is not natural to use for an upper bound in our setting and has not been employed to this purpose until now. Nevertheless, since we aim to bound conditional probabilities, it will prove useful.

Definition B.1

(Disjoint occurrence)
.

Given
{\Lambda}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and two decreasing events
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subset{\Omega}_{\Lambda}
, we say that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}

occur disjointly
in
{\omega}\in{\Omega}_{\Lambda}
if there exist disjoint sets
X,Y\subset{\Lambda}
, such that
{\omega}_{X\cup Y}=\mathbf{0}
;
{\omega}^{\prime}_{X}=\mathbf{0}
implies
{\omega}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
for
{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{\Lambda}
; and
{\omega}^{\prime}_{Y}=\mathbf{0}
implies
{\omega}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
for
{\omega}^{\prime}\in{\Omega}_{\Lambda}
.

Proposition B.2

(BK inequality)
.

For any decreasing events
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subset{\Omega}_{\Lambda}
,

{\mu}(\text{$\mathcal{A}$ and $\mathcal{B}$ occur disjointly})\leqslant{\mu}(\mathcal{A}){\mu}(\mathcal{B}).

We may now start building conditional probability bounds up progressively for segments, parallelograms and, eventually, tubes. For segments, recall
Section

3.5
.

Lemma B.3

(Perturbing a segment)
.

Fix
i\in[4k]
such that
{\alpha}(u_{i})\leqslant{\alpha}
. Let
S
be a discrete segment perpendicular to
u_{i}
and
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\subset S
be discrete segments partitioning
S
. Assume that
|S|\geqslant{\Omega}(W)|S^{\prime\prime}|
and
|S|=q^{-{\alpha}+o(1)}
. Then

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{H}(S^{\prime})|\mathcal{H}(S)\right)\geqslant 1-\frac{W^{1/3}|S^{\prime\prime}|}{|S|}-q^{1-o(1)}.

Proof.

Let us note that a stronger version of this result can be proved more easily by counting circular shifts of the configuration in a
O(1)
neighbourhood of
S
such that a given helping set remains at distance at least some constant from
S^{\prime\prime}
(see the proof of
[
12
]
*Proposition 3.2(3) for a subsequent implementation of this technique). We prefer to give the proof below as a preparation for
B.4
.

By translation invariance, we may assume that
S
is of the form in
Eq.

13
. In view of
3.8
, we need to distinguish cases, depending on whether
\alpha(u_{i+2k})>\alpha
. We first assume that
{\alpha}(u_{i+2k})>{\alpha}
. Thus, helping sets are just
u_{i}
-helping sets or
W
-helping sets. By
3.7
, if
\alpha(u_{i})=0
, there is nothing to prove, since
u_{i}
-helping sets are empty, so
\mathcal{H}(S^{\prime})
always occurs. We therefore assume that
\alpha(u_{i})>0
. We further assume
S^{\prime\prime}\neq\varnothing
, since otherwise the statement is trivial.

Recall from
3.7
that a
u_{i}
-helping set is composed of
Q
translates of the set
Z_{i}
. For
r\in[Q]
we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S)
the event that there is an infected translate of
Z_{i}
by a vector of the form
(r+k_{r}Q){\lambda}_{i+k}u_{i+k}
with
k_{r}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying
Eq.

14
(for
d=0
). Similarly define
\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S^{\prime})
. In words, we look for the part of the helping set with a specified reminder
r
modulo
Q
. In particular, by
3.10
and
3.9
, we have

\mathcal{H}(S)=\mathcal{H}^{W}(S)\cup\bigcap_{r\in[Q]}\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S)

(100)

and similarly for
S^{\prime}
.

Since
|S|=q^{-{\alpha}+o(1)}
, the probability that there are
{\alpha}+1
infected sites at distance
O(1)
from each other and from
S
is
q^{1-o(1)}
. Furthermore, if this does not happen, but
\mathcal{H}(S)
occurs, then all
\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S)
for
r\in[Q]
occur disjointly. Therefore, by the BK inequality
B.2
,

{\mu}(\mathcal{H}(S))\leqslant q^{1-o(1)}+\prod_{r\in[Q]}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S)\right)\leqslant\left(1+q^{1-o(1)}\right)\prod_{r\in[Q]}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S)\right),

(101)

since, as in
3.11
, we have

{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S))\geqslant 1-\left(1-q^{{\alpha}}\right)^{{\Omega}(|S|)}\geqslant q^{o(1)}.

(102)

Using
Eqs.

101
and
100
and applying the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
, we get

\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}(S^{\prime}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}(S))}\geqslant\frac{{\mu}(\bigcap_{r\in[Q]}\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S^{\prime}))}{(1+q^{1-o(1)})\prod_{r\in[Q]}{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S))}\geqslant\left(1-q^{1-o(1)}\right)\prod_{r\in[Q]}\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S^{\prime}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S))}.

For
r\in[Q]
and
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let us denote by
I_{(r)}^{j}
the indicator of the event that
Z_{i}+(r+jQ){\lambda}_{i+k}u_{i+k}
is fully infected and denote by
J_{(r)}(S)
the set of values of
j
such that this set satisfies
Eq.

14
. Since
Z_{i}
has diameter (much) smaller than
Q
, for all
r\in[Q]
, the random variables
I^{j}_{(r)}
are i.i.d. for
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(and therefore exchangeable). Further noting that
J_{(r)}(S)\supset J_{(r)}(S^{\prime})
, and setting
\Sigma=\sum_{j\in J_{(r)}(S)}I^{j}_{(r)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S^{\prime}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S))}

\displaystyle{}={\mu}\left(\left.\sum_{j\in J_{(r)}(S^{\prime})}I_{(r)}^{j}\geqslant 1\right|\Sigma\geqslant 1\right)

\displaystyle{}=\sum_{s=1}^{|J_{(r)}(S)|}\frac{{\mu}(\Sigma=s)}{{\mu}(\Sigma\geqslant 1)}\left(1-\prod_{l=0}^{s-1}\frac{|J_{(r)}(S)\setminus J_{(r)}(S^{\prime})|-l}{|J_{(r)}(S)|-l}\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\frac{|J_{(r)}(S^{\prime})|}{|J_{(r)}(S)|}\sum_{s=1}^{|J_{(r)}(S)|}\frac{{\mu}(\Sigma=s)}{{\mu}(\Sigma\geqslant 1)}=\frac{|J_{(r)}(S^{\prime})|}{|J_{(r)}(S)|}\geqslant\frac{|S^{\prime}|-O(1)}{|S|}.

Recalling that
|S|\geqslant{\Omega}(W)|S^{\prime\prime}|
and
W\gg Q=O(1)
, this entails that

\displaystyle{\mu}(\mathcal{H}(S^{\prime})|\mathcal{H}(S))

\displaystyle{}=\frac{\mathcal{H}(S^{\prime})}{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}(S))}\geqslant\left(1-q^{1-o(1)}\right)\left(1-\frac{|S^{\prime\prime}|+O(1)}{|S|}\right)^{Q}

\displaystyle{}\geqslant 1-q^{1-o(1)}-\frac{O(Q)|S^{\prime\prime}|}{|S|},

concluding the proof for the case
\alpha(u_{i+2k})>\alpha
.

Turning to the case,
\alpha(u_{i+2k})\leqslant\alpha
, there is little to change. Firstly, if
\alpha(u_{i+2k})=0
, the proof above applies, since
\alpha
-helping sets in direction
u_{i}
are the same (since
u_{i+2k}
-helping sets are empty). Moreover, if
-Z_{i+2k}=Z_{i}+x{\lambda}_{i+k}u_{i+k}
for some
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, there is nothing more to prove either, since
\alpha
-helping sets and
u_{i}
-helping sets coincide again. We may therefore assume this is not the case. If
\alpha(u_{i})=0
, then the proof proceeds as above, but with
Z_{i}
replaced by
-Z_{i+2k}
. Finally, if
1\leqslant\alpha(u_{i}),\alpha(u_{i+2k})\leqslant\alpha
, then the proof proceeds as above, but one needs to consider not only
\mathcal{H}_{(r)}(S)
, but also their analogues with
Z_{i}
replaced by
-Z_{i+2k}
.
∎

In the next lemma, we next focus on a parallelogram, which plays the role of one of the hatched ones in
Fig.

4
. Informally, the statement is as follows. The
u_{i}
-side of the parallelogram is of critical size, so that each segment
S_{i,m}
, into which it is decomposed in
4.1
, is also of critical size, allowing us to apply
B.3
to it. The other dimension of the parallelogram is left unconstrained. The lemma provides a bound on the probability that a parallelogram of slightly smaller
u_{i}
-side is traversable (has helping sets for each segment
S_{i,m}
, given that the original one is.

Lemma B.4

(Perturbing a parallelogram)
.

Let
i,j\in[4k]
be such that
j\not\in\{i,i+2k\}
and
{\alpha}(u_{i})\leqslant{\alpha}
. Consider the parallelogram

R=R(l,h)=\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{i}}(l)\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{j}}(h)\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{j+2k}}(0)\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{i+2k}}(0)

for
l\in[{\rho}_{i},e^{q^{-o(1)}}]
and
h=q^{-{\alpha}+o(1)}
. We say that
R
is
traversable
in direction
u_{i}
(
\mathcal{T}(R)
occurs), if for each nonempty segment of the form

S={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\cap R\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}_{u_{i}}(h^{\prime})\setminus{\mathbb{H}}_{u_{i}}(h^{\prime})

the event
\mathcal{H}_{C^{2}}^{\mathbf{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus R(l+W,h)}}(S)
occurs. Let
R^{\prime}=R(l,h^{\prime})
with
1\geqslant h^{\prime}/h\geqslant 1-1/W
. Then

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}(R^{\prime})|\mathcal{T}(R)\right)\geqslant\left(1-\sqrt{W}\left(1-\frac{h^{\prime}}{h}\right)-q^{1-o(1)}\right)^{O(l)}.

Proof.

We start by noting that if
\alpha(u_{i})=0
, there is nothing to prove, since
\mathcal{T}(R^{\prime})
always occurs, so we assume
\alpha(u_{i})>0
. Furthermore, we may assume that
h-h^{\prime}>{\Omega}(1)
, since otherwise either
R\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}=R^{\prime}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
or
R^{\prime}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}=R^{\prime\prime}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
for some
R^{\prime\prime}=R(l,h-{\Omega}(1))
. Let
M=1+\lfloor l/{\rho}_{i}\rfloor
, so that
R
consists of
M
segments perpendicular to
u_{i}
. Let us emphasise that the boundary condition is irrelevant for
\mathcal{T}(R)
, as it is imposed far from the boundary of the domain concerned. Therefore, this event may also depend on the configuration outside
R
.

We partition
R
into its first and second halves
R_{1}=R(\rho_{i}\lfloor l/(2\rho_{i})\rfloor,h)
and
R_{2}=R\setminus R_{1}
. Thus,
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
consist of
\lceil M/2\rceil
and
\lfloor M/2\rfloor
segments perpendicular to
u_{i}
respectively. Recalling
3.7
, we see that if
\mathcal{T}(R)
occurs, then one of the following must occur.

•

The parallelograms
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are disjointly traversable.

•

There is a set of
{\alpha}+1
infections at distance
O(1)
from each other and from both
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
. Furthermore, the parallelograms
R_{1}^{\prime}
and
R_{2}^{\prime}
, formed by removing in each of
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
the
Q
lines closest to their common boundary, are both traversable.

Using the BK inequality
B.2
, this gives

\displaystyle{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R))\leqslant{}

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}(R_{1})){\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R_{2})\right)+q^{1-o(1)}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}(R_{1}^{\prime})\right){\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}(R_{2}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R_{1})){\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R_{2}))\left(1+q^{1-o(1)}\right).

(103)

The last estimate follows as in
Eq.

102
from the fact that traversing the
O(1)
lines at the boundary of
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
happens with probability
q^{o(1)}
together with the Harris inequality
Eq.

7
.

Let us write simply
\mathcal{H}_{m}
for
\mathcal{H}_{C^{2}}^{\mathbf{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus R(l+W,h)}}(R\cap\overline{{\mathbb{H}}}(m{\rho}_{i})\setminus{\mathbb{H}}_{u_{i}}(m{\rho}_{i}))
and similarly define
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\prime}
for
R^{\prime}
. Iterating
Eq.

103
, we obtain

\mathcal{T}(R)\leqslant\left(1+q^{1-o(1)}\right)\prod_{m\in[M]}{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{m}),

since
l=e^{q^{-o(1)}}
. Hence, by the Harris inequality
Eq.

7

\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R^{\prime}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R))}\geqslant\left(1-q^{1-o(1)}\right)\prod_{m\in[M]}\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\prime})}{{\mu}(\mathcal{H}_{m})}.

The last fraction can be bounded, using
B.3
, to obtain

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}(R^{\prime})|\mathcal{T}(R)\right)=\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R^{\prime}))}{{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(R))}\geqslant\left(1-O\left(W^{1/3}\right)\left(1-\frac{h^{\prime}}{h}\right)-q^{1-o(1)}\right)^{M}.\qed

Turning to the proof of
4.11
, recall
Fig.

4
. There the regions introduced in the proof below are depicted as follows. The parallelograms
R_{j}
are North-West hatched, while
R^{\prime}_{j}
are North-East hatched. Thus,
R^{\prime\prime}_{j}
are double hatched. The shaded parallelograms are
R_{j}^{2}
, while
R_{j}^{1}
are the remainder of the area which is North-East but not double hatched.

Proof of
4.11
.

Recalling
4.1
, it is clear that
\mathcal{T}^{{\omega}}_{d}(T)
is the intersection of
2k-1
independent traversability events for parallelograms of length
l
in the sense of
B.4
. Let us denote them by
(R_{j})_{j=i-k+1}^{i+k-1}
and, similarly,
(R_{j}^{\prime})_{j=i-k+1}^{i+k-1}
for
T^{\prime}
with
R_{j}
and
R_{j}^{\prime}
having sides perpendicular to
u_{j}
(see
Fig.

4
). Thus, fixing
j\in(i-k,i+k)
, the role of
i
and
j
in
B.4
is played by
j
and
i+k
here.

Further set
R^{\prime\prime}_{j}=R_{j}\cap R_{j}^{\prime}=R(l-O({\Delta}),s_{j}-O({\Delta}+C^{2}))
.
9
9

9

This equality only holds up to translation, but for simplicity we leave out the translation vector. Note that, although we stated
B.4
for parallelograms with an integer point at one of their corners, analogous bounds hold for non-integer translates thereof.
Notice that (see
Fig.

4
)
R^{\prime}_{j}\setminus R_{j}^{\prime\prime}
consists of two disjoint possibly empty parallelograms
R^{1}_{j}=R(O({\Delta}),s_{j}-O({\Delta}+C^{2}))
and
R^{2}_{j}=R(l-O({\Delta}),O({\Delta}))
with the notation of
B.4
(up to translation). Note that here we used that
R^{\prime}_{j}
has smaller length and height than
R_{j}
, because
s_{j}\geqslant s_{j}^{\prime}
,
l\geqslant l^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}\geqslant d
.

By
4.2
and
7
we have

\displaystyle{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}}^{{\omega}^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\right|\mathcal{T}_{d}^{\omega}(T)\right)

\displaystyle{}\geqslant\frac{{\mu}(\mathcal{W}(T)\cap\mathcal{T}_{d}(T)\cap\mathcal{W}(T^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}}(T^{\prime}))}{q^{-O(W)}{\mu}(\mathcal{T}_{d}(T))}

\displaystyle{}\geqslant q^{O(W)}{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\right|\mathcal{T}_{d}(T)\right),

where
\mathcal{T}_{d}(T)
denotes the event that
T
is
(\cdot,d)
-traversable without boundary condition (also depending on the states of sites outside
T
) and similarly for
T^{\prime}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle{}=\bigcap_{j}\mathcal{T}(R^{\prime}_{j})\supset\bigcap_{j}(\mathcal{T}(R^{\prime\prime}_{j})\cap\mathcal{T}(R^{1}_{j}))

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{d}(T)

\displaystyle{}=\bigcap_{j}\mathcal{T}(R_{j}),

so the Harris inequality
Eq.

9
gives

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\right|\mathcal{T}_{d}(T)\right)\geqslant\prod_{j}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}\left(R^{1}_{j}\right)\right){\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}\left(R^{\prime\prime}_{j}\right)\right|\mathcal{T}(R_{j})\right).

We may then conclude, using
B.4
and that by
3.11

{\mu}\left(\mathcal{T}\left(R_{j}^{1}\right)\right)\geqslant\left(1-(1-q^{\alpha})^{{\Omega}(s_{j})}\right)^{O({\Delta})}.\qed

Appendix C
The surplus factor for balanced rooted models with finite number of stable directions.

To conclude, let us briefly sketch how to remove the
\log\log\log(1/q)
factor appearing in
8.5
, which would also propagate to pollute
1
(e)
(see
Eq.

82
).

Theorem C.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be balanced rooted (classes
(b)
and
(e)
). Let
{\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}
be as in
Section

8.1
. Instead of
8.4
, one can define
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})})
in such a way that

\displaystyle{\gamma}\left({\Lambda}^{(N^{\mathrm{int}})}\right)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\log^{O(1)}\log(1/q)}{q^{\alpha}}\right),

\displaystyle{\mu}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{1}}({\Lambda}^{(N^{i})}))\geqslant{}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{-1}{{\varepsilon}^{2}q^{\alpha}}\right).

Sketch proof of
C.1
.

To prove this, one should combine the techniques of
Sections

7.1
and
8.1
. More precisely, a bound on
a_{m}^{(n)}
less crude than
Eq.

68
should be established along the lines of
Eq.

49
. As in
Eq.

60
, we may further decompose
a_{m}^{(n)}
into a product over scales
p\leqslant n
.

The relevant values of the parameters correspond to
m
such that
(3/2)^{m}\leqslant 1/(\log^{W}(1/q)q^{\alpha})
, say, and
p\in[N^{\mathrm{cr}},n]
, as other cases can be dealt with using the crude bound
Eq.

68
. Further, as in
Eq.

63
, we can also discard
p\geqslant N^{\mathrm{cr}}+\Psi
. Hence, we need to focus for the remaining values of
m
and
p
on lower bounding

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}_{p}\left(\left({\Lambda}^{(p+1)}\setminus D_{1}\right)+s_{m}\right)\right|\mathcal{T}_{p}\left({\Lambda}^{(p+1)}\setminus D_{1}\right)\right)

(104)

and
{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}((D_{1}\setminus D_{0})+s_{m})|\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}}(D_{1}\setminus D_{0}))
, the latter being treated exactly like
{\mu}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}|\mathcal{T})
in
Eq.

61
.
Equation

104
can be further decomposed as a product over elementary regions delimited by the boundaries of the
(D_{\kappa})_{{\kappa}\in[K]}
(recall
Figs.

14
,
8.2
and
9
).

Unfortunately, for such (non-convex) polygonal regions
R
, bounding

{\mu}\left(\left.\mathcal{T}_{p}\left(R+s_{m}\right)\right|\mathcal{T}_{p}(R)\right)

is no easy feat. Indeed,
4.11
only treats tubes and, more importantly deals, with helping sets for one direction only in each part of the tube (recall
Fig.

3(a)
), while
\mathcal{T}_{p}(R)
requires helping sets in various directions, which are all dependent. To make matters worse, for certain families
\mathcal{U}
it may happen that a single set of
{\alpha}
infections is simultaneously a helping set for different directions and this would create complex and heavy dependency among different directions, which could,
a priori
, make boundary regions attract such sets.

To deal with this issue, one could further elaborate
8.1
. Indeed, we may split
{\Lambda}^{(p+1)}\setminus D_{1}
into disjoint horizontal strips (recall
Fig.

14(b)
) of width
\ell^{(p)}/(W{\varepsilon})
. Each strip is assigned a direction
u_{j}
,
j\in(-k,k)
and we only ask for helping sets for this direction to be present. These requirements are again cut at a small distance from the boundaries of all
D_{\kappa}
into parallelograms like the ones treated in
B.4
. We further demand
W
-helping sets on segments close to the boundaries of the various
D_{\kappa}
as in
8.1
. Naturally, some leftover regions remain without helping sets as in
8.3
, but they are unimportant like in
Section

8.1
.

By doing this, we make the event
\mathcal{T}_{n}(R)
the intersection of traversability events of parallelograms in the sense of
B.4
, so that its result can be applied as in the proof of
4.11
, leading to a calculation similar to the one in
7.3
. The only significant change is that now there are
O(W\ell^{(p+1)}/\ell^{(p)})
parallelograms instead of a constant number. This is not really a problem. However, if we wish to avoid careful computations, given that we are interested in the range
p\in(N^{\mathrm{cr}},N^{\mathrm{cr}}+\Psi)
, we can brutally bound
W\ell^{(p+1)}/\ell^{(p)}
by its maximum, which is
\log^{O(1)}\log(1/q)
by the definition of
\Psi
,
Eq.

62
.
∎
